2026 Brookfield Affiliates Investor Day: Fireside Chat with Bruce Flatt and Howard Marks

Brookfield(13.6K)·37:51·2026-10-01
Bruce FlattCEO, Brookfield Howard MarksCo-Chair, Oaktree
TL;DRBrookfield CEO Bruce Flatt and Oaktree Co-Chair Howard Marks discussed the evolution and resilience of private alternative asset management, emphasizing an ownership mentality, rigorous downside protection, and the strategic advantages of deploying…
capital counter-cyclically...

TL;DR

Brookfield CEO Bruce Flatt and Oaktree Co-Chair Howard Marks discussed the evolution and resilience of private alternative asset management, emphasizing an ownership mentality, rigorous downside protection, and the strategic advantages of deploying capital counter-cyclically during market dislocations.

Journey and Evolution of Alternative Assets

  • Pioneering High Yield and Alternatives: Howard Marks recounted starting at Citibank in 1969 when investing was restricted to stocks and bonds, later being assigned in 1978 to figure out high-yield bonds ("the first alternative investing"), which subsequently paved the way for private equity in the mid-1980s, venture capital in the 1990s, hedge funds, and private credit.
  • Oaktree's Founding: In 1995, Marks and four co-founders left TCW managing $7 billion to launch Oaktree, driven simply by the belief that good performance would sustain them, eventually growing to approximately $230 billion today.
  • Brookfield's Operating Roots: Bruce Flatt explained that Brookfield emerged from an operating perspective rather than purely financial engineering, managing real estate, infrastructure, and renewables for over 125 years and utilizing outside capital to scale without diluting common shareholders.
  • Early Innings and Consolidation: Flatt noted that private asset management is still in its early innings and will continue to grow for another 10, 20, or 30 years as capital piles up globally from sovereign funds, pensions, and private wealth. Simultaneously, industry consolidation is driving large institutional allocators to concentrate relationships with massive, multi-product platforms like Brookfield and Oaktree.

Ownership Mentality vs. Hired Hands

  • Brookfield's Skin in the Game: Marks highlighted that unlike typical "hired hands" running pieces of paper, Brookfield commits its own capital directly alongside clients and listed entities, guaranteeing alignment of interest.
  • Buying the Unpopular: Marks praised Brookfield’s willingness to buy assets for its own account that no one else will touch ("nirvana in the investment world"), noting that the long-term backbone of Brookfield is acquiring cash-flowing businesses with strong downside protection.
  • Scale and Exclusivity: Flatt emphasized that in asset classes like power, infrastructure, and real estate, greater scale yields more exclusivity, less competition, and more certain or superior returns.

Case Studies in Counter-Cyclical Investing

  • Oaktree's $11B 2008 Deployment: In October 2008—amid the collapse of Lehman, Bear Stearns, and AIG—Oaktree sat on the largest distressed debt fund in history ($11 billion, 90% uninvested). Reasoned that if the world ended, investing wouldn't matter; if it didn't, not investing meant failing their job. Bruce Karsh invested an average of $450 million a week for 15 weeks ($7 billion in one quarter).
  • Brookfield's Enron Hydro Purchase: In 2002 following Enron's bankruptcy and total chaos in the power markets, Brookfield invested $900 million in hydro plants in Maine and upstate New York, which required patience and doubling down before eventually being refinanced and yielding multiple times their money.
  • The Westinghouse Acquisition: Brookfield leveraged its 50+ years of power market expertise and proprietary intelligence—recognizing that the plants' operating grids could not shut down without crashing regional power grids—to underwrite a worst-case, downside-protected 15% runoff return, with massive upside from electrification and AI.
  • The Rules of Conviction: Flatt stressed that successful contrarian investing requires proprietary information, a fortress balance sheet to survive early losses or falling knives, and institutional backing (board, partners, and investment committee) to avoid panicking during drawdowns.

Due Diligence and Risk Control

  • Exclusionary Filtering: Flatt stated that most potential deals are rejected within an hour or two (throwing out 8 out of 10 incoming deals immediately) because Brookfield's existing portfolio ($1.3 trillion of assets) provides unmatched proprietary pricing data.
  • Credit as a Negative Art: Marks cited Graham and Dodd's view that fixed income is a "negative art" where lenders have no upside, only degrees of failure; thus, Oaktree’s foundational motto is "if we avoid the losers, the winners take care of themselves."
  • Underwriting the Borrower: Both leaders emphasized putting themselves in the shoes of the borrower to rigorously evaluate survival capability and worst-case recovery outcomes before committing capital.

Macroeconomics, Inflation, and Interest Rates

  • Historical Perspective on Rates: Marks argued that current rates are not historically "high," but rather "in the zip code of normal," having only appeared elevated relative to the zero-rate policy maintained from 2009 onward to rescue the financial system.
  • Natural vs. Administered Rates: Marks welcomed a potential return to natural interest rates set through direct negotiation between borrowers and lenders rather than decades of Fed-administered rates.
  • Spread Compression on Borrowing: Flatt noted that equity strategies earning 12% to 25% are largely indifferent to a 5% vs. 6% base rate, and because lenders have compressed spreads from 400 basis points down to roughly 100 basis points since the zero-rate era, actual borrowing costs have not risen as drastically as headline rates suggest.
  • Geopolitical vs. Structural Inflation: Flatt and Marks agreed that current inflation (around 3.4%, down from 9.5% in 2021-2022) is primarily driven by oil prices and two ongoing wars rather than classic "too much money chasing too few goods," meaning it is transitory rather than permanent. Consequently, markets are currently punishing sound assets due to flawed assumptions that high inflation and rates will last forever.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Bruce Flatt: Predicts that private asset management will continue its inexorable shift into private hands for another 10, 20, or 30 years due to the relentless accumulation of capital in sovereign wealth funds, pension funds, and private wealth.

Notable Quotes

  • > *“Being too far ahead of your time is indistinguishable from being wrong.”* — Howard Marks (quoting an adage from the 1972-1973 era)
  • > *“The cop who caught [Son of Sam] passed away a few years ago and in his obituary it reported that they said to him, 'How did you catch the son of Sam?' And he said, 'I put myself in position to get lucky.'”* — Howard Marks (referencing Brookfield's approach to opportunistic investing)
  • > *“In real life, things fluctuate between pretty good and not so hot, but in the minds of investors, they go from flawless to hopeless. And you would rather buy when most people are depressed and think it's hopeless.”* — Howard Marks
Howard and Bruce, the last time we were together was in April when Barron's tapped you as the most likely investors to succeed Warren Buffett as the Oracle of Wall Street. You took very different paths to get here and I thought we could actually start by going back and hearing a little bit about your journeys and decisions to form asset management companies, and we'll start with you, Howard. Well, you know, when I started full-time work at City Bank in 1969, investing consisted of stocks and bonds and the city's results in stocks were so bad and I was part of it that I was banished to the bond department in 1978. And just in time to get a phone call from the head of the bond department in August saying, "You know, there's some guy named Milken or something in California who deals with something called high yield bonds. Can you figure that out?" And that was, you know, ostensibly the first alternative investing because it was so non-mainstream. Bond investors could only buy investment grade. And then of course the financing from high yield bonds led to private equity, which really got birthed—you know, a few people did a little in the mid-70s, but it got birthed in the mid-80s for the most part. And then, you know, the next big move I think was venture capital in the 90s because, you know, in '99 VC investments went up hundreds of percent. Then of course there was the tech bubble burst and then hedge funds had their day because they didn't go down too much in the crash because of the long-short nature, and then of course private credit. So it's been a great evolution. I was very lucky to be in at the beginning of that process in '78. So, you know, at City I did high yield and converts. Then I went to TCW in '85 to do high yield and converts and I was approached by a guy named Bruce Karsh who had an idea. He says, "Why don't we do a distressed debt fund?" So we did in '88, and that was a great place to be. And then, you know, Bruce and I and our other three co-founders from TCW had the chance to go out in '95 and start our own company. And you know, we just felt that we had done a good job for our clients and if we could continue to do so, we would have a success on our hands. We were managing $7 billion at the time in '95 at TCW. And we never asked ourselves whether we would ever get back to seven billion, but then this process that I described took root and alternatives began to be talked about around '03-'04 in the wake of the tech bubble burst, and you know, today we're at 230 or something. And Bruce, how did your trajectory align with what Howard's just described? Look, I would start by saying I think we're still in the very early innings of what Howard described. The business continues to go into private hands inexorably. It's happened for, I'm going to say, 30 years, Howard, and it's going to happen for another 10, 20, 30 years. And the reason for that is because what we generally do is buy assets or businesses and own them and don't have to worry about the distractions of the public markets. And if you look at any of the securities we own, we own great businesses within them and assets and just run them like they're private businesses. And I guess that's—I say all that because with the amount of money piling up internationally in sovereign funds, pension funds, wealth for private clients, etc., this is not stopping. That's the first thing I'd say. If I go back to answer your question, we didn't know that would occur 30 years ago, but we thought what we had was an edge and we had a bunch of businesses or operations in real estate and infrastructure and renewables. And that the only way we figured to grow those businesses was to bring in outside capital to do it. We didn't want to dilute the common shareholder. The only other way to do it at that time was to do it with debt, which wasn't a good idea as lots of people go bankrupt that way. Therefore, we came upon the alternatives industry, and it was early, early days but we got lucky in being in the middle of that and really created the asset management business out of that. So I'd say it was the same trends but different because we came at it from an operating perspective. We just didn't know how to manage money. We just wanted to run the things that we did and do them well and do them at scale because probably the most important thing that we fell upon is that with the things that we do—power, infrastructure, real estate, all the types of infrastructure, and even our industrial businesses—the bigger you get, the more exclusive it gets, the less competition you have. The returns are either better or more certain. And that was probably the thing that we finally figured out, and that's why we scaled the business to the extent we did. I think the thing that really distinguishes Brookfield, the more I've gotten to know it over the last seven years, is this ownership mentality of not being a hired hand who runs pieces of paper for somebody else, but somebody who owns assets and tries to optimize their value. And I think Howard, we could not have—I think back to when we first talked about partnering on Oaktree. If we didn't have similar ethos about value investing and downside protection and all the things, we probably couldn't have got along, right? Because we are different. The firms are different in some respects, but in some respects they're similar. And that gave us the basis of being able to create a partnership that worked. Agreed. And when you look at alternative finance, the industry now has really grown exponentially recently. What do you think sets you all apart? And if you could just talk a little bit about the changes you've seen in just the past five years even. So I think the thing that is going on in the industry today, number one, like I said, it's getting bigger and bigger and bigger. It is not stopping and more money's coming into privates; that's going to keep happening. But like everything, consolidation happens and therefore all those groups that have a trillion dollars or $2 trillion dollars, they can't manage it. They can't give random people hundreds of millions of dollars because it's not meaningful to them and they can't keep track of it and they can't keep relationships. So what's happening is the large groups in the world and the large groups that control capital are concentrating their relationships. That was part of the reason we didn't have credit within Brookfield to any major extent, and therefore adding Oaktree into it allowed us to have a much more broad platform to be able to deliver to those clients because we could deliver all the way across the spectrum. And I'd say that has differentiated all the products that we have, but now with our other managers, it has allowed us to differentiate our product in a way that I won't say nobody, but not many people in the world that deal with institutional, retail, and wealth investors have the breadth of product that we have. And that has given us and will give us in the future an enormous advantage to be able to invest. But you know, when you say differentiates, Katherine, I keep coming back to the ownership mentality. When Brookfield is making transactions for its clients or its listed entities, it's also committing its own capital to the same investments, and that guarantees the alignment and benefits from Brookfield's expertise and operations. And the other thing that I think stands out—again, I've been a director since the beginning of 2020—what I've seen is, as at Oaktree, the willingness to buy something for your own account that nobody else will buy. And nirvana in the investment world is to find such a thing that other people won't touch with a 10-foot pole, assuming you're right, of course. But the hired hand might say, "Well, I don't know. I don't know if I want to do that because it might be unpopular with the clients." But the person who's committing their own capital says, "I want to do it because it's something I believe in that I'm getting at a really attractive price because everybody else is allergic to it." But Howard, I recall after the first I'm going to say five board meetings you came to at Brookfield, you said to me one day because we describe deals, and you said to me something to the effect of, "I didn't realize your private equity business was actually what you do. This is a real business. You're buying things with downside protection and cash flows, and these are incredible long-term businesses that you can own in the company." And I think that's been the backbone of what we do: not to get complicated and just own great businesses in great places with great people. Well, you know, when Buffett talks about value investing, I think the first words out of his mouth are, "These are not pieces of paper. These are businesses we want to own for the long term." And that's that distinction—not pieces of paper. And I think one other similarity you all share is your ability to invest counter-cyclically and to spot the diamond in the rough. Could you each talk about some examples of that? Well, you know, when I think about willingness to go against the grain, if you will, the best example is October of '08. Here we are. We've lost Merrill Lynch, Bear Stearns, Wachovia, Washington Mutual, AIG; now Lehman goes under. People are talking about the end of the financial world. And we have just raised $11 billion in a distressed debt fund, 90% of which is sitting on the shelf. The biggest distressed debt fund in history was $2.5 billion. We raised $11 billion for our '07-'08 fund. Should we invest it? Simply, should we invest it? What if the world ends as people were talking about? What if all the financial institutions melt down? Well, we made a very simple calculation. We had no facts to go on. We had no prior experience with the meltdown of the financial system. And we simply concluded that if we invested and the world ended, it wouldn't matter. But if we didn't invest and the world didn't end, then we didn't do our job. So it turned a complex decision into a very simple one. And we reasoned that it's hard to predict the end of the world. It's hard to give it a high probability. It's hard to know what you would do if the world ended. And most of the time the world doesn't end. So, in reflection that seems very simplistic, but that's all you had since you didn't have data or prior experience. And so, Bruce Karsh took that decision process and invested an average of $450 million a week for the next 15 weeks. That's $7 billion in one quarter, and that's really all you had to do. So that's just an example; simplistic for you, but not for the rest of the world. Bruce, Westinghouse seems to be a good example of something Brookfield saw very early, that saw the potential very early. Yeah. So look, many of you know this in the room, but I would say what we always try to do—and the only way to do it when you're dealing with markets like that is because it's unknowable—you try to have more information about certain things than almost everyone else. If you do that, you'll have more confidence when the time is ready to make a decision to invest. And secondly, you need to have fortress amounts of money around such that you can confidently make those decisions and not have to second-guess whether you're going to be in business after you made those decisions. And third, you have to have the governance and the people and the organization to back your decisions so that on the way through those decisions, you don't do something stupid. Because often people make decisions like that and then they second-guess themselves, and that's where they get themselves in trouble. And often it's not the first investment that actually works the best. The first investment, sometimes the best ones, they go down by 50%. And then you have a really tough decision: do I double down or do I quit? And when you double down, that's often the best investment you've ever made because you got into it, it was the right decision, you just missed the timing. Once in a while—and I'll come to Westinghouse—once in a while it's different. But there are just many, many pieces, many real estate investments we've made over the years where we're just too early. The knife was still falling. You never know when the knife's going to hit, right? One of the greatest adages that I ever learned was around '72, '73, and somebody told me that being too far ahead of your time is indistinguishable from being wrong. And it's true, but you have to live with that. You want to be ahead of your time, but there are consequences. You have to cope. But it actually doesn't matter as long as you're in the investment and you have enough capital to keep doubling down and you keep at it, provided you had good information upfront. You can come through the bottom—like we bought Enron when Enron went bankrupt. The power markets were a total nightmare. I think that was 2002, total disaster. We bought $900 million—which at the time was a lot of money—of hydro plants in Maine and upstate New York. It was tough for a long time, but eventually we refinanced all of our money out and we made a fortune out of those investments; it was many, many times X. So it takes patience, but we had to bear with it and keep putting money into it and double down. I'd say that's the real trick. But to do that you have to be prepared upfront and have money. But more importantly, you have to have backing. Everybody has great ideas, "I'm going to double down," but if you don't have the capital to do it or you don't have the backing of your partners, your shareholders, your investors, your board, your investment committee, it can go bad. Now, Westinghouse—look, that one was unusual. We had probably an added benefit. When I go back to what I just described, we have been in the power business the whole life of this company, 125 years plus. But in the last 50 years, we've been invested in the power business a lot in every single type. At that time, we were in quite a few. And we had more knowledge about the power markets. We had one, to go back to Howard's thesis, we had one piece of information that nobody else had. They could have had it, but they didn't act on it. The piece of information was that each of the power markets for most of where these plants were in the world could not shut down; if you shut them down, the grids would go down. Which meant that they were staying running for the duration of the life of those plants. And if you did that, you'd earn a downside-protected 15% return. That was our "blowdown" scenario. So when we sat back and looked at it, if your worst-case scenario is you're going to earn 15% runoff, that's pretty good. And the rest is history. Power markets have come along, electrification has come along, AI has come along, etc. Westinghouse has been very successful for three of our businesses, two funds, and one of our renewables public entities. But if you didn't have those things, you wouldn't have been in a position to do it. But just to belabor it a little further, I think the essence of good investing is to kind of cover the downside and arrange your affairs so that the surprises are on the upside. And I know I've told you the story before, but in New York back in the '70s, we had a multiple murderer called the Son of Sam. The cop who caught him passed away a few years ago and in his obituary it reported that they said to him, "How did you catch the Son of Sam?" And he said, "I put myself in position to get lucky." And whenever I hear you tell the Westinghouse story, you bought something that was out of favor. You didn't have to beat off a lot of other bidders to get it. You had the capital, you had the long-term view, but you also had a belief because of the piece of information you described that in the worst case you're going to make 15% a year. And the upside from there was— And I would say that's nine out of 10 times that's all I ever focus on when our people bring investment decisions to us at an investment committee. It's: what's the downside? I don't care about the upside. If you're in 15, 20, 22, 25, 35, 40—you get lucky and earn seven times your money, whatever it is—all those are just bonus scenarios. Just don't lose money and earn us 12% in a downside scenario. And we talk about the congruence between values at Oaktree and Brookfield. From its first day, Oaktree operated under the motto that if we avoid the losers, the winners take care of themselves. To which I would say the one thing that we were careful of is that credit can be historically a dangerous place to be in capital markets. The reason why Oaktree was appealing to us is they had an ethos of downside protection, and therefore we knew that we weren't going to get ourselves in trouble by backing them because they knew how to make credit decisions. That was probably the most important thing for us in entering into the partnership. To the question about how to avoid losses, Bruce, at the investor days in New York, you asked everyone to talk about the investments you've passed on. Talk a little bit about your due diligence process and what makes you walk away. Most things we do upfront, within an hour or two we can decide whether we want to do it. Most things we buy, it's knowable what they are; usually we understand them because we have a business like it. Within 15 minutes I can make a decision on whether we should do that investment or not. The rest is all confirmatory due diligence. Once in a while we toss an investment out because something came up or there was something that changed the theory, but out of 10 investments that come in, we toss out eight right away because they're not in our margin of what we do, or we don't know enough about them, or we don't want to go there, or we have too many of them, or we got a duplicate one—whatever it is, we throw them out. The two remaining, within a very short period of time, we can decide, "Yeah, that price makes sense for that." Because we can price the thing. When you do what we do—we have $1.3 trillion of stuff—we know more than most people about all those things. And when someone brings it to us, we know the values, so we can throw them out right away. The rest is just confirmatory due diligence. That's not to say that everybody who works with us isn't important because they are. But it's not me making the decisions; it's us as a group. We only do things where we have more information to apply to the decisions than others. And that allows us to toss out the bad ones first. And often, because we do so many different things—and Howard can talk about this—when too much money pushes into markets, it's not that they're not good assets, it's the price of them is too high. And that often means assets are excellent, they're just not priced properly when they're on offer to you, which is why buying in tougher markets is so important to long-term returns. Same situation for you at Oaktree, Howard, in making the decisions not to invest? Well, of course, Oaktree has two basic businesses. Number one, as a lender, and the calculus is a little different for a lender because a lender has no upside. Either you get paid or you don't. There are no different degrees of success; there are only different degrees of failure. So the only thing that really matters is that you get paid. Graham and Dodd in "Security Analysis" called fixed income investing a "negative art." You maximize your returns not by what you buy, but by what you exclude. So we have a little different calculus. But we also have what I call "aspirational businesses"—distressed debt, power, infrastructure, and special situations are the best examples—where we're trying for returns, let's say north of 15, maybe north of 20. And there you actually have to find winners. But we still put the importance of risk control first in our philosophy because we want people to have that front of mind. It's easy to get intoxicated about how much money you can make if things go right, but you must not make an investment until you ask, "What happens if things go wrong?" Clearing that hurdle is the necessary condition, I think, for putting together a portfolio where the surprises are on the upside. But even in the credit funds, Howard, when we're making a loan, we're putting ourselves in the shoes of that borrower and saying, "Will this company survive? Can they pay our debt? Will they make it out the other side?" And if they don't, what are the outcomes? That's the analysis we make of every single amount of money that we provide out of the business. And coming to today's market, there's no dearth of uncertainty, excitement, and angst in the markets. From where you're sitting, what do you think the market is getting right and what is it getting wrong? Well, I think the market is getting right the fact that AI is likely to be the biggest thing any of us have ever seen and will totally change our way of life. Another thing the markets are getting right nowadays is the fact that interest rates have been too low and higher rates were called for. In the last 10 days or so we've had a big run-up in rates, and now everybody's running around with their hair on fire. Everybody says, "What do you think about these high rates? What do you think about higher for longer?" The first thing I always say is these are not high rates. They're high relative to the rates that have prevailed since '09 when the Fed decided to cut the Fed funds rate to zero to save the world financial system, but these are not high rates. These are quite low rates relative to the long sweep of history, and you might say they are in the zip code of normal. I think that the rising rates accurately reflect the fundamentals, and that's a good thing. Everybody in this room, I think, believes in the free market system, and we invest in private enterprise to make money. We haven't had a free market in money in maybe 30 years; we've had administered rates from the Fed. I think there's a higher probability going forward that interest rates will be what they call natural interest rates, which are the rates that are set through negotiations between borrowers and lenders. What better source of interest rates than that? So I think that's good. Now, what the market may be getting wrong: everybody has to reach their own conclusion about how to invest in AI and how to do it safely. Everybody has to make a judgment: do you want to make as much money as the most aggressive investor in AI makes, or are you happy with a little less to do it securely? And of course, people have to figure out the import of these new higher rates. Will they choke off the economy or not? I believe they will not, but that's just my opinion. I don't bet too heavily on that. And higher rates for your business, for the credit business, isn't necessarily a bad thing. You wrote in your memo "Sea Change," which was four years ago, that the change to a higher, more volatile rate environment was a bit like coming out of the wilderness for you all. Around 2012, 13, 14, 15, I used the same title for my repetitive speech: "Investing in a Low Return World." Lenders and creditors were in a low-return world. How do you make a decent return in a low-return world? It's very difficult. The best way to do it is to either take more risks to get less return than you used to get, or use leverage—neither of which is attractive. So we didn't do it. We settled for low returns, but that doesn't make your business fun. I think the important thing, Katherine, is that interest rates are really the environment we live in as investors, like air for animals. It's key in all of our businesses and it has differential effects. As an asset owner, especially someone who owns assets on borrowed money, you want to see rates that are low and declining. As a lender, you want to see rates that are high and maybe rising. And the most important thing for the investor, I think, is to have a diversity of those so that the portfolio is balanced. You don't want to have all the assets in your portfolio that respond in the same direction to a given change in the environment. I think that our businesses are an offset to each other in that regard. Bruce, what are you thinking about what the market is getting wrong and the impact of higher rates? I'd just say I agree with Howard. Related to equity strategies, we earn low-end 12%, high-end 25%. Whether you pay five or six doesn't matter; this is irrelevant. It's better to pay five than six. And secondly, don't forget we don't borrow. If you're a country, you issue bonds and you get that Fed rate, but what we tack on is a spread. And spreads when interest rates were zero were 400 basis points. Today, as interest rates are five, they're 100. So a huge amount of it has been taken out of the spread you pay. The amount that's getting paid by a borrower is different and it's higher, but it's not that much different from when rates were zero because people just said, "I'm going on strike. I'm not lending below four." Today they aren't on strike. They are saying, "Okay, I'll reduce it to six." So there's this, it is higher, but it's not that much higher. Second, I guess we should all remember: there's too much inflation caused by oil prices and two wars going on in the world. Those are going to end. If you look at inflation in the rest of the system, it's not that much. And so we shouldn't get—I think everyone should just consider: do not get distracted by one form of inflation, which is something that was created and will go away at some point in time. The interesting thing about that is the classic reason for inflation is too much money chasing too few goods. That has nothing to do with the inflation of today. Correct. Today's inflation is more geopolitical than anything else. And the other thing is today's inflation is 3.4%. Back in '21-'22 it was 9.5%. So it's not that high. Exactly. And I think that's probably the most important thing. Everyone thinks inflation's going up. Do you know what you should be thinking? Inflation is not going up; it's going to be down at some point in time. Therefore, take advantage of that situation. That's the message: the news today is yeah, there's inflation, but that's not going to be here forever and do not make decisions based on that. So, does that translate to now being a good buying opportunity for some things? It's an excellent opportunity because they're getting punished in the capital markets, because the common thread out there is that inflation's here forever, interest rates are going up forever, and they're not. And what are those things? BBU, BIP, and BEP, since we're here to talk about those three today. Well, look, Katherine, I'll just wrap up. Annie Lamont: $14B Managed, 70+ Exits, 15 IPOs, 7x Midas Investor

Sourcery with Molly O'Shea(74.1K)·62:04·4.5K views·2026-10-01
Annie LamontCo-Founder and Managing Partner, Oak HC/FT Molly O'SheaHost, Sourcery Andrew AdamsCo-Founder, Oak HC/FT
TL;DRAnnie Lamont, Co-Founder and Managing Partner of Oak HC/FT, shares insights from managing $14B across a 40+ year career, highlighting the convergence of AI with healthcare and fintech, and the growing existential threat posed by China capturing 50% of pharma's outside research dollars.

TL;DR

Annie Lamont, Co-Founder and Managing Partner of Oak HC/FT, shares insights from managing $14B across a 40+ year career, highlighting the convergence of AI with healthcare and fintech, and the growing existential threat posed by China capturing 50% of pharma's outside research dollars.

Oak HC/FT Fund Model & Strategy

  • Firm Evolution and Fund Size:
    • Started with a $500M fund to deploy quickly; recent funds have grown to $2B to meet larger capital requirements of modern companies.
    • Acts as a life-cycle investor, deploying check sizes ranging from $1M up to $100M from day one.
  • Investment Allocation:
    • Early-stage and seed investments traditionally oscillate between 20% and 40% of the fund ($400M to $800M).
    • Maintains the flexibility to invest up to 10% of the fund in common stocks (e.g., PIPEs in healthcare/biotech).
  • Venture Building & Repeat Founders:
    • Approximately 15% of the firm's work involves ideating and building companies from scratch (e.g., CareBridge, Main Street Health, Augur).
    • Actively backs repeat founders with high integrity, product/tech orientation, and deep market insights (such as Todd Park and Brad Smith).

AI Transformation in Healthcare & Fintech

  • Horizontal Platforms in Regulated Sectors:
    • Oak HC/FT targets horizontal platforms applied to healthcare and fintech, which together comprise 50% of the economy.
    • Deep sector expertise helps navigate the "smallest large market" (e.g., 100 key providers and 10 large payers).
  • Administrative Cost Reduction:
    • 25% to 30% of all healthcare costs are administrative, representing a primary target for AI-driven automation to lift burdens off clinicians.
  • Clinical and Diagnostic Improvements:
    • AI tools act as critical verification layers (e.g., assisting radiologists who misread 30% of images and allowing patients to leverage tools like ChatGPT).
    • Robotic surgery vision models are projected to surpass human sight limitations during procedures.

Life Sciences, Drug Discovery, & The China Threat

  • The Explosion of Life Sciences AI:
    • After a decade of tracking AI in biotech without viable developable products, the advent of specialized biological and chemical models (along with lab robotics) has radically accelerated drug design.
  • China's Research Dominance:
    • Pharma companies now spend 50% of their outside research dollars in China—a dramatic shift over the last 4 years.
    • China matches or exceeds U.S. university output in fundamental patents, creating an existential threat to the U.S. pharmaceutical and biotech ecosystem amid stagnant NIH funding.
  • Chai Discovery's Roadmap:
    • Backed by Oak HC/FT last year, Chai Discovery achieves 100x higher lab productivity in drug design.
    • Roadmap sequence: Antibodies (already nailed) -> Peptides (making material progress) -> Small molecules.

Portfolio Spotlights: Devoted Health, Haluminate, and Augur

  • Devoted Health:
    • Started 10 years ago by Todd Park, the Medicare Advantage health plan has tripled in size over the last year, halved its operating ratio, and dramatically increased EBITDA.
    • Integrates an AI frontend, the Devoted Medical Group primary care network, and proprietary payer technology platforms.
  • Haluminate:
    • A reinforcement learning environment creating agents that build financial and economic models, mimic investment analyst workflows, and simulate entire corporate ecosystems (e.g., real estate brokers).
    • Scaled from zero to 60 in 6 months.
  • Augur:
    • Founded by Dave Clark (former Amazon supply chain and e-commerce head with 22 years of experience), backed by a $100M upfront commitment from Oak HC/FT.
    • Provides real-time supply chain analytics and operational execution agents for Fortune 500 clients, currently scaling in its second year.

Notable Quotes

"The amount of their operating ratio. They have dramatically increased their EBITDA. And you have a company that is I would say almost impossible to compete against in the future... It is definitely the most AI, probably the best example of AI applied to healthcare in the world right now." — Annie Lamont (on Devoted Health)

We have an existential threat in this country, in an industry that is being co-opted, and we need to through technology and tools and investment and a complete re-engineering of our FDA and processes, fix that rapidly." — Annie Lamont
"The bar for your entrepreneurs is not high enough." — Jerry Gallagher (recalling advice given early in her investing career)

CMU Miami | Interview with Ken Griffin

Carnegie Mellon University(170.0K)·54:57·2026-10-01
Ken GriffinFounder and CEO, Citadel
TL;DRKen Griffin announced a historic $3 billion philanthropic commitment to Carnegie Mellon University—the largest individual gift in higher education history—funding a $1 billion investment in Pittsburgh (including naming the Kenneth C.
Griffin School of Computer Science) and a $2...

TL;DR

Ken Griffin announced a historic $3 billion philanthropic commitment to Carnegie Mellon University—the largest individual gift in higher education history—funding a $1 billion investment in Pittsburgh (including naming the Kenneth C. Griffin School of Computer Science) and a $2 billion expansion to establish CMU Miami.

The Historic $3 Billion Commitment & Allocation

  • Total Investment: $3 billion commitment by Ken Griffin (Founder and CEO of Citadel), recognized as the largest individual philanthropic gift in the history of higher education.
  • Pittsburgh Campus ($1 Billion):
    • Kenneth C. Griffin School of Computer Science: Receives $500 million to advance computer science leadership.
    • University Priorities: The remaining $500 million supports highest institutional priorities, including faculty and student talent recruitment, financial accessibility, and multidisciplinary research.
  • CMU Miami Campus ($2 Billion):
    • Scale and Timeline: Located on over 35 acres in the heart of Miami (specifically Wynwood), designed to serve more than 3,500 undergraduate and graduate students at full scale by 2041.
    • Educational Model: Organized around societal challenge areas—such as human health, climate resiliency, energy, national security, cybersecurity, and advanced manufacturing—rather than traditional academic majors or departments.

Speakers & Participants

  • Farnam Jahanian: President of Carnegie Mellon University, co-architect of the partnership and campus expansion vision.
  • Ken Griffin: Founder and CEO of Citadel, philanthropist, and primary donor.
  • Josh Shapiro: Governor of the Commonwealth of Pennsylvania, highlighting CMU's historical legacy and economic impact on the state.
  • Corey O'Connor: Mayor of Pittsburgh, emphasizing the city's industrial roots and technological future.
  • Dave McCormick: U.S. Senator for Pennsylvania, providing video remarks on higher education and regional growth (*unable to attend in person due to a Senate floor vote*).
  • Meredith Meyer Grelli: Vice Provost for Entrepreneurship and Associate Vice President, serving as stage moderator for the fireside chat.
  • Additional Key Figures Mentioned: Cason Carter, Julia Quinn, and Alex Slocum (Citadel colleagues); Alex Laskey and Andre Dua (McKinsey team supporting the effort); David Coulter (CMU Board Chair); Jim Garrett (Provost); Marshall Hebert (Dean of the Griffin School of Computer Science).

CMU's Legacy of Innovation & Economic Impact

  • Historical Innovations: Alums and researchers created foundational technologies, including CAPTCHA tests, Kevlar fiber, and JavaScript code. Home to the world's first Robotics Institute (>50 years old), the first drama school (67 Tony Awards, plus numerous Emmys and Oscars), and the nation's first Computer Science College.
  • Pittsburgh Tech Ecosystem: Features 250 high-tech companies in the local ecosystem (including 100 in robotics and 100 in AI). Notable alumni/spin-off unicorns highlighted include GECKO, Bridge, Skill AI, and Duolingo.
  • Corporate R&D Footprint: Major global corporations maintaining high-tech and deep-tech operations in Pittsburgh due to CMU's ecosystem include Microsoft, Google, Caterpillar, Honeywell, and Bosch.

Philosophy & Higher Education Context

  • Addressing Skepticism: President Jahanian and Ken Griffin discussed headwinds facing higher education, emphasizing that bold institutional leadership requires rejecting caution ("hunkering down") in favor of solving hard problems.
  • The "Who" over "Where": Griffin emphasized that institutional excellence is driven by people rather than geography, noting the cultural and entrepreneurial parallels between Pittsburgh and Miami (both driven by grit, immigrant ambition, and hard work).
  • Cross-Disciplinary Integration: CMU Miami is explicitly designed not as a traditional branch campus or hub-and-spoke model, but as a deeply integrated extension where faculty and students collaborate across traditional academic boundaries.

Notable Quotes

*"Today is, I think, an incredibly important day for the United States. We have lost sight of the narrative of just how damn important higher education is to our country... That is the thesis behind the single largest investment that I have made in my lifetime."* — Ken Griffin
*"Instead of organizing their classes around traditional academic majors, students are actually going to be forced to focus on how to solve the pressing problems of today... They'll invent new technologies around energy, ensure our communities are resilient in the face of a changing climate, and prepare our nation for a future of advanced manufacturing."* — Josh Shapiro
*"Where is far less important than who. With the right people, you can make anything happen wherever you want to, but you need the right people."* — Ken Griffin
*"CMU is really good at cross-disciplinary, multidisciplinary explorations, both in education as well as research... Here's the blank slate to experiment, envision it, and hopefully that will become a model for the rest of the world to follow."* — Farnam Jahanian

From deepfakes to DNA: the science of watermarking AI

Google DeepMind(928.0K)·38:10·6.9K views·2026-10-01
Hannah FryProfessor, UCL Pushmeet KohliVP of Science, Google DeepMind Jeremy RatcliffeResearch Scientist, Google DeepMind
TL;DRGoogle DeepMind's SynthID watermarking technology—developed over eight years for digital media like text, images, and video—has now expanded into biology as "SynthID Bio" to trace the provenance of AI-generated protein sequences and 3D structures without impacting their biological function.

TL;DR

Google DeepMind's SynthID watermarking technology—developed over eight years for digital media like text, images, and video—has now expanded into biology as "SynthID Bio" to trace the provenance of AI-generated protein sequences and 3D structures without impacting their biological function.

Core Concepts of AI Watermarking

  • Defining the Goal: According to Pushmeet Kohli, watermarking provides a sense of provenance and lets users verify whether content originated from a human, nature, or an AI system.
  • Key Requirements: Imperceptibility (invisible to users), robustness (survives attacks, crops, transformations, or noise), and scalability (easy to integrate and detect across systems).
  • Flaws of Traditional Classifiers: Declining Accuracy: Machine learning classifiers that look for traditional tells (like overused words) lose accuracy over time as generative AI models improve.
  • The Text Watermarking Mechanism: Optionality & Secret Keys: When a large language model generates text, it chooses from a distribution of valid words. A secret background key biases certain word choices, allowing detectors to identify AI generation through statistical patterns across a chain of words.
  • Limitations in Text: Short Snippets: Very short text inputs (e.g., "What is the capital of France? It is Paris") cannot be effectively watermarked due to a lack of variable options.
  • The Image/Video Watermarking Mechanism: Co-Trained Networks: A neural network modifies the unwatermarked image subtly to embed a signal. It is co-trained with a detector network and tested against an adversarial agent that crops, shrinks, rotates, or adds noise to ensure the watermark remains robust.

SynthID Bio and Biosecurity Applications

  • Dual Biological Offerings: Jeremy Ratcliff explains that SynthID Bio includes SynthID Bio Structure (predicting protein structures on top of AlphaFold 3 by shifting atom positions and angles) and SynthID Bio Sequence (introducing watermarks into amino acid strings).
  • The Biosecurity Threat: Bypassing Screening: Bad actors can use AI to design custom protein sequences that look completely different in sequence space from restricted pathogens in DNA synthesis screening databases, but still fold into harmful structures.
  • The Solution for DNA Synthesis: Provenance Confidence: When a DNA synthesis company receives an order, watermarking allows them to verify that a sequence originated from an AI system with built-in safety guardrails rather than an unknown source.
  • Protein MPNN Analogy: Sequence Generation: SynthID Bio Sequence builds on protein MPNN (a third-party tool that goes from structure to sequence), taking advantage of allowable swaps between similar amino acids (like leucine and isoleucine) that maintain identical structure and function.
  • Wet Lab Validation: Hit Rates: Laboratory tests on watermarked protein binders showed near-identical hit rates and quantitative binding measures compared to unwatermarked binders, proving functionality is preserved.

Industry Adoption and Future Integration

  • Ecosystem Expansion: SynthID is already utilized across the industry by companies like Nvidia and OpenAI, and detection is built directly into apps like Gemini.
  • Regulatory Alignment: Pushmeet Kohli notes that generative content watermarking has been brought into law within the EU, with regulators recognizing it as a scalable solution for provenance.
  • Open-Source Biology Strategy: SynthID Bio is being released as open-source code to catalyze conversations with model developers and DNA synthesis screening companies, allowing them to exchange keys and implement the technology without central coordination.
The Podcast. I'm Professor Hannah Fry. Picture the scene. You are scrolling through your socials and you see some remarkable footage, the up-close, slow-motion eruption of a volcano or a tornado raging through a village or perhaps some footage of a protest. Is that real? Was this made by human or a machine? Well, as AI gets better at generating text, images, audio, and video, that is becoming one of the most challenging questions of our time, except that now the answer just got a lot more complicated. We are entering a world where AI can design biological molecules, structures that have never existed in nature before, some of which could be designed to cause harm. So, how do you stop something dangerous before it gets made? Well, the answer, it turns out, might be hidden in plain sight, embedded invisibly into the very thing you're looking at or the very molecule you're about to synthesize: a watermark. Not the blocky logo you see on a stock photo, something far more subtle, something mathematical. And today I am joined by two people who are working on this from different angles. Pushmeet Kohli is VP of Science at Google DeepMind and one of the architects of SynthID, the watermarking system now being adopted across the AI industry. And Jeremy Ratcliff is a biosecurity researcher whose team has applied that same technology to biology. Welcome to the podcast, both of you. Jeremy, let's start with you. Watermarking anything that AI spits out, I mean, it sounds like a very good idea, but just break it down for me. Why is this helpful? So for biology, sort of the motivation for being able to watermark is so that we can have provenance and say specifically where did this sequence come from. And really what we're trying to differentiate is between sequences that come from things in nature versus sequences that come as products of AI systems. So specifically, the type of risk that we're thinking about in terms of AI systems is sort of an AI-generated sequence that can have properties that we know to be problematic, but we might not be able to identify from the sequence itself that it has these problematic properties. And what makes a good watermark? Because I think when people consider the phrase, they sort of think of, I don't know, like bank notes or like passports. Is there like rules for what counts as a good watermark? The thing for us, in particular, human imperceptibility is one, so making it very difficult for someone to be able to tell what portion of data has been watermarked... because otherwise it would be really easy to erase. Yeah. Having high detectability, so having some system that can go through and actually identify the watermark with high confidence, and then also good generalizability, so being able to have at least a watermarking method that can work across a bunch of different systems rather than having to make bespoke ones for every single data type. What do you think, Pushmeet? I mean, does that apply beyond biology and more broadly? Yeah, I think the main reason why we started the whole watermarking project at DeepMind almost eight years ago now was to give a sense of provenance to what users are seeing. If you look at different modalities, whether it's text, whether it's images, whether it's videos, it was hard to imagine 10 years back that we would today be talking about systems that can create images, videos, audio, text, and proteins or enzymes that are indistinguishable from what a human or what exists in nature. And it's an important sort of element for users to give users this idea that you can really believe what you're seeing. So, what do we need from a watermark? We need three properties. One, it should not degrade the quality, otherwise basically the whole point is lost, people will not use it. The second element is robustness against attack or against transformations. If people want to remove it, it's hard to remove in the sense that even if you make changes to the signal, it will persist. And then the third requirement is that it should be easy to use, right? It should not, it should be easy to integrate in the signal and it's easy to detect from the signal. So those are the three sort of properties of imperceptibility, robustness, and scalability that we have designed all our watermarking systems for with the whole idea that users have more control and have a better view of what they are seeing or what they are sort of using or designing and so on. Feels like this subject of watermarking has been quite a big deal this summer, but at the same time we're a few years into generative AI being accessible to the public. What's taken so long, Pushmeet? Why is it only now that this has become so widespread? When image editing software started becoming very sophisticated, people were sort of very concerned about this issue of is this a real image or has this been tampered with? And there was a lot of work and specialized systems which could really sort of analyze an image and think and see the tiny differences that exist in a tampered image and can say, "Oh, yeah, this was a fake image or this has been tampered with," and so on. But as generative AI became more sophisticated, those tiny differences went away. And then that naturally sort of resulted in the question, "Is this game over in the sense that humans will not have the ability to detect whether something is AI-generated?" And this is where watermarking sort of came in. We said the way to sort of make sure that humans always have the ability to understand the origin of where some signal came from is by injecting a signal within it, having a bias, so it doesn't go away. Even if the models are perfect, and they have the ability to sort of generate images which are indistinguishable from what a camera would observe or what a text that a human would write, we specifically sort of put in some imperceptible things that can allow users to later detect that this originated from an AI model. And when you say "we" here, Pushmeet, you really do mean "we" in the literal sense, right? It was you, it was you guys that came up with a solution for this one that was scalable? Yes, so our team has been working on this for almost eight years. We started with images, because people were concerned about these images being used for fake news, misinformation, and so on. And so there was that motivation to solve that problem. At the same time, although the problem is extremely challenging because of the properties we just mentioned of robustness and imperceptibility and efficiency and scalability, still an image is a very large amount of data. A one-megapixel image has sort of 1 million sort of numbers or like 3 million numbers depending on how it's encoded and so on to hide information, right? And without changing the content. So we developed watermarking systems for images first. Later on, we had to look at the challenge of how do you do this for other modalities, like text, which are not as high-dimensional as images, where actually it's a very small amount of signal, right? If you look... So 3 million numbers in a sentence, is there? Yeah, exactly. It's a few words. You can't change those sort of few words, and you have to be very cautious in terms of not changing the meaning of the content. And so the approaches that we developed for watermarking text were quite different and distinct from what we use for watermarking high-dimensional signals like images and videos and audio even. But this idea of watermarking which you guys pioneered has now been brought into law. It's essential that any generative content within the EU has to have some sort of watermarking. I think what regulators and countries around the world have recognized that they want to give users this information as to the origins of the content that they are consuming, and one sort of key technology that is seen as a robust solution for it that can scale is watermarking, and which is why not only have we seen a take-up of this idea across the industry, the fact that SynthID is used by Nvidia, is used by other partners like OpenAI, and so on, it's a remarkable sort of thing that many of our partners in the generative AI space have adopted this technology. In images, what happens is we take the image which has not been watermarked, and then there is a neural network which looks at that image and modifies it in a very subtle way to incorporate the signal. And this particular model which is incorporating the watermark is being co-trained with a detector neural network, and they are both sort of trying to achieve the following: you get an image which is unwatermarked, the watermark generator injects a very small signal which is imperceptible. So if it is perceptible, we say, "That's not great. Users will see it, and that degrades the quality of the image." Then in the middle, there is an adversarial agent which is going to try to make modifications to the image.
