We have plenty more to come on this series, but in the meantime, check out our other episodes and we will see you very soon.

How did a few hundred Spanish soldiers topple two empires? – Si Sheppard

Dwarkesh Patel(1.4M)·97:24·137.5K views·2026-10-01
Dwarkesh PatelHost, Dwarkesh Patel Si SheppardMilitary Historian
TL;DRHernán Cortés and Francisco Pizarro conquered massive American empires with a few hundred conquistadors, leveraging profound technological differentials, superior tactical adaptability, devastating introduced diseases, and extensive alliances with…
local indigenous populations...

TL;DR

Hernán Cortés and Francisco Pizarro conquered massive American empires with a few hundred conquistadors, leveraging profound technological differentials, superior tactical adaptability, devastating introduced diseases, and extensive alliances with local indigenous populations who were chafing under Aztec and Inca rule.

1. Global Context and Early Spanish Expansion

  • The Global Economy: In the 15th century, Europe was cut off from Eastern spice and silk markets, forcing innovations like Prince Henry the Navigator's use of wind currents and tides to turn the Atlantic into a highway.
  • The New World Discovery: Christopher Columbus sailed west in 1492 aiming for Oriental markets and accidentally discovered an unknown continent full of potential wealth, resources, and people.
  • The Expedition Blueprint: In 1511, de Velázquez seized control of Cuba and began sending coastal expeditions, laying the groundwork for more ambitious penetrations into the interior.

2. Hernán Cortés and the Conquest of the Aztec Empire

  • The Rise of Cortés: Assigned by Velázquez in 1518 to explore Mesoamerica, Hernán Cortés—a former chief magistrate (*alcalde*) with legal training—exceeded his mandate and launched an expedition to penetrate and seize the interior.
  • Alliances with Subjugated Peoples: Upon arriving in Mesoamerica, Cortés identified that local groups like the Totonacs chafed under Aztec hegemony; after fighting the Tlaxcalans to a standstill, he forged a crucial alliance with them for guides and reinforcements.
  • The March to Tenochtitlan: Cortés entered the Aztec capital and met Moctezuma II, eventually taking the Great Speaker under protective custody, burning regional officers alive in front of him, and governing through a puppet monarch.
  • Internal Spanish Strife and Recovery: Governor Velázquez sent Pánfilo de Narváez to arrest Cortés; Cortés defeated him, absorbed his forces, and returned to the capital only to find a popular revolt that led to *La Noche Triste* (The Tragic Night).
  • The Final Siege: Rebuilding his forces with tens of thousands of indigenous allies, Cortés constructed prefabricated brigantines (transported over mountains by porters), mined sulfur from Popocatépetl for gunpowder, and took control of Lake Texcoco to reduce Tenochtitlan by 1521.

3. Francisco Pizarro and the Fall of the Inca Empire

  • The Andean Expedition: Inspired by Cortés's playbook, Francisco Pizarro secured funding, sailed south, and arrived in Peru in 1532 during an Inca civil war between the late Sapa Inca's sons, Atahualpa and Huáscar.
  • The Battle of Cajamarca: Atahualpa met Pizarro with tens of thousands of troops; Pizarro unleashed artillery, musketry, and shock cavalry in a confined courtyard, slaughtering thousands of Inca while suffering zero enemy casualties (and only a single self-inflicted cut to Pizarro's hand) and capturing Atahualpa.
  • The Siege of Cuzco: Pizarro's puppet ruler, Manco Inca, later rebelled, laying siege to Cuzco and cutting off coastal reinforcements, but tactical errors (such as attacking Lima across open ground and failing to sustain relentless night assaults) doomed the native resistance.

4. Military Dynamics: Technology, Tactics, and Limitations

  • The Conquistador Advantage:
    • Steel Armor and Weapons: Toledo steel thrusting swords and steel helmets easily defeated local wooden and obsidian weapons.
    • The Horse: Decisive shock cavalry that could blast through loose native formations and retreat faster than enemies could pursue.
    • Fighting Dogs: Armored war mastiffs added terrifying psychological impact.
  • Native Countermeasures: Both Aztecs and Incas adapted by fighting in urban environments, breaking lake dikes to flood cities, building man-sized holes in buildings to evade horses, and attaching Spanish swords to poles to improvise pikes and use bolas.
  • Cultural Constraints on Warfare: Pre-Columbian societies fought to capture victims for religious sacrifice rather than to kill, making them reluctant to eliminate leaders on the ground and hampering their strategic flexibility.

5. Demographics, Governance, and Long-Term Outcomes

  • Hierarchical Vulnerability: Both the Aztec and Inca empires were centralized autocracies governed by a tiny elite; capturing the single apex ruler destabilized the entire administrative structure.
  • The Demographic Catastrophe: Europeans introduced Old World diseases like smallpox, to which native populations had zero acquired immunity, leading to catastrophic long-term demographic collapse.
  • Economic Impact on Spain: The influx of American gold and silver funded European wars against the Ottomans and Protestants, but imported severe inflation, depressed real wages, and prevented Spain from developing functional capitalism.
  • Global Trade Integration: Silver from mines like Potosí in Bolivia was shipped across the Pacific to Manila, allowing the Spanish to trade excess liquidity for Chinese silk, spices, and porcelain, finalizing the first truly global trade network.

Notable Quotes

"If you allow this man inside your house, he will take your house away from you." — Cuitláhuac (warning his brother Moctezuma II about allowing Cortés into Tenochtitlan)
"My men and I suffer from a disease of the heart that only gold can cure." — Hernán Cortés (explaining his motivation to Moctezuma's envoys)
"Atahualpa was planning to have Pizarro for lunch, but Pizarro ended up having him for breakfast." — Si Sheppard (describing the Battle of Cajamarca)
"We play the lottery for that exact reason. But be careful what you wish for, because you may just actually get it." — Si Sheppard (on the corrupting, short-term lure of easy resources and bullion)
Today I’m chatting with professor and military historian Si Sheppard. I want to talk to you about the conquest of the Aztec and the Inca Empires because these are some of the most shocking events in human history, unparalleled among all the military and diplomatic achievements ever made. A few hundred conquistadors show up to a new continent they know nothing about. Within a matter of years, they subjugate empires which have millions of inhabitants in them. In the process of doing so, they are often winning battles where they are outnumbered 100 to 1, 1,000 to 1, and in many cases not even taking a single casualty. They’re doing this while upwards of 99% of their fighting forces are made up of the very natives that they’re going to subjugate. I wanted to have you on to talk about something this episode. We’re not going to focus on it, we’re mostly talking about history here, but people think of AI takeover as this crazy sci-fi scenario but this would not be the first time in history that an alien force, with motivations that the natives don’t understand, is able to use diplomatic cunning and technological advantages to outmaneuver and then take over a much larger and well-established order. I wanted to have you on to understand what happened here. First, I would love to hear the stories. What did Cortés do? What did Pizarro do? How did these conquests actually go down? Thank you. It’s a great pleasure to be here. Yes, we’re talking about the birth of our modern world, which played out between 600 to 500 years ago and created the modern globalized community that we all participate in today. It began in the 15th century when Europe, backed up against the wall of the Atlantic, under immense pressure from external forces and cut off from the core economic power bases of the East—the sources of the spice and silk that had driven the global economy historically throughout Eurasia—was finally forced to innovate. Key names like Prince Henry the Navigator of Portugal work on turning the Atlantic Ocean from a barrier into a superhighway. Generational knowledge accrued from captains reporting on tides and wind currents and whatever land they’ve managed to reconnoiter, building out the map as they go along, enables them to find a way around the southern tip of Africa directly to India and therefore cut out the middleman. The Spanish, meanwhile, are heading west. We all know the story of Christopher Columbus: "In 1492, he sailed the ocean blue." That was entrepreneurship. This was an expedition to arrive at the Oriental markets by traveling west. Of course, his figures were completely wrong. He was saved by the fact that, unknown to him, there was an entire continent in the place where he thought Japan, China, and the Spice Islands would be. Initially, that was perceived, ironically, as a disappointment. But over time, it became increasingly apparent this was a source of potential in land, in wealth, and in people that could be exploited. A key turning point happened in 1511 when an ambitious Spanish nobleman, de Velázquez, seizes control of Cuba. He sends out from this power base expeditions exploring the coast of what we would call Mesoamerica, Central America. Later in 1518, he assigns that task to a man with whom he’s had a somewhat tempestuous relationship, a man named Hernán Cortés. He’d arrived in the New World in Cuba in 1511. He had some legal training, so he served as a notary and worked his way up to being alcalde, which is chief magistrate, on Cuba. But he had much greater ambition. He takes this warrant from the governor and runs with it. He begins recruiting men to a much wider and much more ambitious goal: not just to explore the coast, but to penetrate into the interior, to find out what’s there, and to seize control of it. When the governor realizes what Cortés is up to, he tries to stop him. There’s this great moment on 18th November 1518. He arrives at the waterfront just as Cortés gets into a boat to go out to his ships offshore. The governor calls out to him, "What are you up to?" Cortés assures him, "Don’t worry. I have this all under control." But of course, the governor had lost control at that point. Now it was Cortés’s expedition. This is the background. Again, as you say, he is literally going off the side of the map. He’s entering terra incognita, unknown to Europeans previously. His political antenna, honed by years in the islands, enables him to appreciate at a very early stage that there is a significant imperial power which has hegemony here in the mainland, and that its subject peoples chafe under the authority of whoever this lord or imperial power is. He arrives in the lands of the Totonacs, who had recently been incorporated into the imperial orbit of a people who call themselves the Mexica—hence Mexico as we know it—but who have come down to history based on their mythical origins in a land called Aztlán as the Aztecs. For simplicity’s sake, that’s what we’ll refer to them as. The Totonacs resented this authority, as all subjugated and subordinated peoples do, and they saw the arrival of these outsiders as a potential opportunity to throw off the authority that had been imposed on them. Having established this relationship with the Totonacs, Cortés begins to march inland, and he enters the territory of the Tlaxcalans, who, like an autoimmune system, immediately rallied to defeat him, to expel him from their territory. But he fights them to a standstill for such a period of time that the Tlaxcalans and he arrive at a new kind of arrangement. The Tlaxcalans again appreciate his strength, his power, his potential. They swing over to his side and will henceforward serve as guides and reinforcements for his expedition deeper into the interior. Can we pause here and talk about the numbers? How many men had Cortés brought with him? Even the Tlaxcalans had much larger numbers than Cortés did. So even the standstill is an impressive military achievement, especially because he’s fighting without native support. What are the numbers even at this point? A few hundred on Cortés’ side and multiple thousands on the Tlaxcalans’ side. It’s at this point that the critical technological differentials from the Old and New World really begin to come into effect. The classic text which got me invested in this whole scenario in the first place is Jared Diamond’s book, Guns, Germs, and Steel. He enumerates in the title those critical advantages that the Spaniards had. Guns in reality weren’t so significant. Once the Native peoples got over the shock value, they appreciated, given the very slow reloading times involved in firearms at that time, that they weren’t so significant as assets on the battlefield. But steel was critical. Local weapons were carved from wood and from stone and from obsidian, that volcanic glass which is razor sharp when honed to a point and quite dangerous as a slashing weapon. But against the steel armor, and especially the steel helmets that the conquistadors were wearing, it was much less effective. The conquistadors were drawing on military traditions of the Old World that went back generations, incorporating long Toledo steel sword blades. They used those as thrusting weapons to take out indigenous warriors who were coming in with cut-and-slash weapons, which of course open up the side of the body to be taken out very early. The combination of the technology with generations of inherited military training and skill meant that the conquistadors had that critical edge on the battlefield. Now, they weren’t invincible. Cortés was again playing those mind games. He was taking casualties, and he was careful to bury his dead underneath the houses that they were sheltering in so the Tlaxcalans wouldn’t be aware that they were even having some successes in hand-to-hand combat. It was as much a mind game as it was a physical game. The ultimate weapon, of course, that the conquistadors brought with them was their horses. I cannot overemphasize how vital the horse was to the conquistadors once they entered into the interior of the American continents. They had the external asset. They had sail power, which enabled them to land anywhere they wanted on the coast. But in the interior, the horse was decisive. There were no native equivalents. The horse originated in North America, but it had subsequently died out. The local peoples had not just no horses, but no beast of burden generally. That was not integrated into their economies, into their ways of life, or into their military. The Inca, whom we’ll discuss in a moment, did have one, which was the llama. When they campaigned, trailing for miles behind their armies would be these endless baggage trains of pack animals, llamas. The Aztecs had no equivalent. Their only domestic animals were the turkey and the Chihuahua. Speaking of dogs, the conquistadors also brought these big war mastiffs, these fighting dogs which wore armor and were trained to take down men. Again, imagine the terrifying impact that would have for the indigenous people. But the horse was the critical asset. On attack, you could blast through any native formation which didn’t have not just the weapons, but the training or discipline to be able to stand up to massed shock cavalry. If things were going bad for the conquistadors, they could retreat much faster than, of course, the enemies could chase them. The Tlaxcalans appreciate all these assets coming together, and they see this as their opportunity. They were the holdouts, the last remaining major entity in Mesoamerica which was still able to go toe-to-toe with the Aztecs. They’d been completely surrounded. The Aztecs were upping the ante every year with these ceremonial flower wars, which were becoming more and more obviously wars of conquest. The Tlaxcalans had been cut off from the coast and from sources of salt. So they were being pushed to the brink. This is a Hail Mary for them. "If we ally with these outsiders, these strangers, who are just few in number after all, we may have some potential way to break out of this strategic trap that has been imposed upon us." They lean over to the side of Cortés, and they travel with him to Tenochtitlan, the capital, the glittering jewel in the midst of Lake Texcoco, the capital of the Aztec Empire. Again, this is a dramatic moment when Cortés passes through the causeways out into the city and encounters Moctezuma II, the huey tlatoani as he was called, the Great Speaker, the emperor of the Aztec Empire. These two worlds come together at that moment. The interface between them was Cortés’s interpreter/mistress, Doña Marina, Malinche as she became known, who is translating from one side to the other. The dialogue that occurs then is one of those great tipping points in history. What was the perspective of Moctezuma II? He was aware Cortés was coming. This is his empire. He has envoys and emissaries and scouts and spies everywhere. They’re reporting back to him, "Something has washed up on the shores of our empire, and it is determined to meet with us." He initially bargains with Cortés, urging him to leave. He provides him with precious gifts, which of course just inflames Cortés even more. Once he’s got hold of Aztec gold, he’s just determined to go all the way to the center. Moctezuma continues playing this double game. His advisors, his council, urge him not to allow Cortés to proceed to the capital. His own brother, Cuitláhuac, tells him, "If you allow this man inside your house, he will take your house away from you." But Moctezuma proceeds, and for that he’s been condemned by generations of historians. This was a mistake to bring the conquistadors into the heart of the empire itself. I actually think that was his best option, whether he was aware of it or not, because it was better for him to have Cortés as his guest within Tenochtitlan than it was to have Cortés, this random element, at loose within his empire, talking to the subject peoples, inflaming them possibly against central authority. He felt perhaps Cortés was what he said he was, an envoy from a great emperor on the far side of the vast ocean. Cortés, again with the mind games, had no warrant to say he was a diplomat or ambassador for anybody. He kept telling anyone who’d listen, "I speak for the mighty Charles V, the Holy Roman Emperor." Of course, Charles V had no idea who Hernán Cortés was. Cortés, again, is just winging it, just incredible improvisation here. And for a moment… stasis. The great turning point comes when word arrives from the coast that one of the Aztec regional military officers, on his own initiative, has taken action against the conquistadors left behind on the coast, the ones with the Totonacs, and some of them have been killed. Cortés seizes this as his pretext. He marches into the palace of Moctezuma II, the Aztec huey tlatoani, and tells him that for his own safety, he is now being taken under the protective custody of the Spanish, and he should come with him, or else there will be violence. This is the moment when Moctezuma’s entire worldview just collapses. Nobody had spoken to him like this before. His psychology just disintegrates at that point, and he meekly allows himself to be led away. He tells his people, "Don’t resist. I don’t want bloodshed. I will pass with these strangers." Then once Cortés has rounded up the officers from the coast responsible for turning on his men there, he has them, in front of the emperor and the entire court, burned alive, which is a form of punishment totally unknown to the Aztecs. They watch this in grim silence. Once Cortés has seized the emperor himself, once he has Moctezuma, he already has the empire. He governs through what is increasingly becoming his puppet. Moctezuma seems to have fallen almost under the spell of Cortés, the classic Stockholm syndrome. He gives him a grand tour of the city. Cortés, in again a subtle flex of his power, has brigantines, small sailing boats, built. He takes Moctezuma with him on a sailing cruise on Lake Texcoco, and the emperor’s astonished at this new technology of using the wind to sail on the water. Having the emperor was the key. The emperor’s mistake wasn’t inviting Cortés into the city. It was making himself vulnerable by staying there also in an adjacent palace. If he had gone to some further location, made some excuse, a pilgrimage or an expedition of some kind, and had ruled through subordinates, Cortés would’ve been baffled. He couldn’t go chasing after the emperor. That would be obviously too provocative. But without the emperor there, Cortés would have had his options extremely limited. I don’t know what he would’ve done in that circumstance. But because Moctezuma stayed in his capital, he made himself the target. Once he was taken, this was the key to controlling the entire empire at that point. Obviously the story continues from there, with La Noche Triste. What people might not appreciate about what happened here, in both the Pizarro story of the Inca Empire which we’ll talk about later, and in the Aztec story, is that in both cases the Spanish were themselves divided and in fact fighting each other. So maybe you should tell the story of how Velázquez sends another expedition to capture and arrest Cortés for going beyond the prerogative of his mission. We left the governor on Cuba, of course, fuming. He rallies another expeditionary force because Cortés is stealing his thunder. He sends this under Pánfilo de Narváez, a larger force than the one Cortés brought with him originally. This lands on the coast. Cortés appreciates he has to move, so he leaves one of his subordinates, the hot-blooded Alvarado, behind in charge in Tenochtitlan, takes as many men as he can to the coast, confronts de Narváez, and defeats him in a minor battle. Again, diplomacy wins over the entire expeditionary force that the governor had dispatched. He tells them, "My friends, why are we fighting? We have the empire at my disposal. Join with me and we can share in the riches." So he wins over the vast majority of this new force. It effectively becomes reinforcements, and he marches back to the capital. He finds the mood has turned against him. The problem with being in charge is you can’t be everywhere. His subordinate, assuming that the Aztecs were mobilizing against him, decided on a preemptive strike. There was a festival. The aristocracy, particularly the warrior caste, were celebrating, and Alvarado just descended on them and massacred everybody. The survivors rally the population. When Cortés returns, he finds himself effectively under siege. The people are so appalled now with the failure of their huey tlatoani that when he tries to pacify them, they throw stones at him. Mortally wounded, he either dies or the Spanish finish him off, because he’s no longer of any use to them. Now Cortés is trapped in Tenochtitlan. He determines to break out, and that is, as you say, La Noche Triste, the Tragic Night, because so many of his men are left behind. They’re either killed as they try to escape the city across the causeways or forced to retreat back to the palaces, where they’re taken alive and sacrificed subsequently. Cortés gets away with a minority of the troops still at his disposal and makes it back to Tlaxcalan territory. That was the critical aspect, the two features of the entire campaign. One, he has enough strike power with enough horses to defeat the Aztecs when they try to finish him off, at the Battle of Otumba. They feel he’s on the run. They have the numbers. The conquistadors are dragging their tails between their legs as they flee. Cortés himself is grievously wounded. "We can finish them off." They had the numbers. They were winning. But again, Cortés identifies where the Aztec general is. He rallies enough horsemen to blast through the Aztec ranks and take out their command and control. The Aztec army disintegrates, and the Spanish are able to flee. The battlefield advantages, their horses, their steel, were critical, but just as critical was the diplomacy. If the Tlaxcalans had turned on Cortés at that point, he would’ve been finished. Not one Spaniard would’ve survived. None of them would’ve made it back to the coast. But the Tlaxcalans, for their own reasons, not out of any generosity or humanitarian impulse to welcome back the conquistadors, still see, "We are committed to these people and to our alliance." So they give Cortés shelter. He recovers physically, begins to rebuild his strength, and is determined to go back to Tenochtitlan for round two: "This time we’re not going to be indulging diplomatic niceties." The Tlaxcalans have their own perspective on this. If you go to Tlaxcala, the territory today, the governor’s mansion there has murals on the wall, and these depict Cortés and the conquistadors from the Tlaxcalan point of view. It’s fascinating to look at how the relationship is not between a superior and an inferior. It’s between equals. You see them depicted in the same scale, engaged in diplomacy between partners, not between subordinates and dominant figures. The Tlaxcalans drove a hard bargain. "We want territory carved out of the Aztecs. We want our own fortress in Tenochtitlan after this is over." They saw this as them using the conquistadors, not the other way around, which is very important to bear in mind. The Aztec Empire from a distance, if you look at a map, seemed impressive. But this was an imperial construct, and not just the remaining independent entities like the Tlaxcalans, like the Tarascans, who we can discuss later, were hostile to the Aztecs. So were so many of their subordinated peoples. The Totonacs on the coast, the Chalco states on the shores of Lake Texcoco, they chafed under Aztec hegemony and authority. They too saw the conquistadors as an opportunity to finally shrug off the authority and the demands of the Aztecs, and get revenge in a lot of ways. Cortés builds up for the final campaign against Tenochtitlan, against the new huey tlatoani, Cuitláhuac, and then, after he died, his brother, the last of the huey tlatoanis, Cuauhtémoc. He has indigenous people flocking to his banner from all over Mesoamerica. He emphasizes that he’s still in the fight. He still has those advantages. More reinforcements are arriving at the coast, and they’re being integrated into the conquistador army. But of the force that marches on Tenochtitlan, the conquistadors were maybe 0.5% in raw numbers. The overwhelming majority were other Mesoamerican peoples who see this as their final reckoning with the Aztecs. This is the integration again of the technology with the diplomacy. Cortés knows that to take the city, he has to control the lake. Again, the innovations… They need sulfur for their gunpowder. Their guns are useless without gunpowder. So an expedition goes into the mountains to the volcano, Popocatépetl, and they dangle with ropes inside the caldera of the volcano, scooping out the sulfur they need as the critical ingredient for their gunpowder. Another one builds prefabricated brigantines, boats, in their different sections, and then has untold numbers of porters, human load-bearers, bring these prefabricated pieces over the mountains to the lake. They’re assembled and let loose in the lake, and now they have control over the lake. Bringing together the technology and the innovations with the local manpower, this is the force directed against Tenochtitlan in the final battle. I actually didn’t know those stories. That’s crazy. One of the ways in which, to the extent people are even aware of the story, they might not understand what actually happened, is that they have this model of the Aztecs or the Incas as this cohesive single nation. A better model might be something like, imagine Russia and Ukraine have been doing trench warfare for 100 years. Imagine the animosity, and then also the Russians are taking tens of thousands of Ukrainians and doing human sacrifices with them. Then some foreign force comes in and says to the Ukrainians, "We can help you march into Moscow." I think they’d have pretty decent odds of getting some agreement there. The old phrase is, "The enemy of my enemy is my friend," and in diplomacy and war, that’s always very true. The key term there, as you used it, was dispersal. The vulnerability of the Aztec and the Inca was that hierarchical nature of their society and the absolute loyalty that the system had invested into whoever was atop the social hierarchical tree: the huey tlatoani for the Aztecs, the Sapa Inca for the Inca themselves. Taking that individual destabilizes the whole system and potentially even means that it can be functionally governed through the use of intermediaries or even puppets. The dispersed peoples, as you say, the Comanche, even the Maya in Mesoamerica, whose city-states jealously asserted and defended their autonomy against each other, meant that the Spanish couldn’t take out the Maya in one fell swoop. They couldn’t do to the Maya what they’d done to the Aztecs and the Inca. They had to reduce their cities one by one. That was a long, laborious process that took almost to the end of the 17th century to finally consummate. Even then there was still smoldering dissent. In many ways, you can go to the highlands of Central America and they still speak the ancestral languages as the first one before they speak Spanish. Yes, colonizing powers can find aggressive bite-back. Just look at Custer’s Last Stand as late as 1876 here in America, the entire 7th Cavalry wiped out at the Little Bighorn because they lost the initiative. At that point, they were fighting warriors who had come together temporarily, riding horses, firing guns, and then dispersing afterwards. It was very hard to pin them down. That was often a frustrating aspect. It took a long process of what amounts to really total war — setting up barbed wire everywhere and of course slaughtering the buffalo to take away the basis for their culture and their food chain — to finally bring the last holdouts to heel. So it can be a long, protracted process. The Inca and Aztecs themselves found that out. They never consummated even incorporating their entire zones within their empires. The Aztecs had surrounded and were slowly reducing the Tlaxcalans. But to the west, in what’s Michoacán, the Purépecha people, or as they’re called today, the Tarascans, had an empire of their own, and they were able to go toe-to-toe with the Aztecs. The first time the Aztecs crossed into Tarascan territory, it was a disaster. A key reason for that was actually technology. The Aztecs had no connection with South America. The Tarascans somehow did. We know from linguistic evidence and costume choice there was some exchange of flora and fauna, and above all in technology. The Tarascans could smelt metal. Like the Inca, they could smelt copper, and they used that to form the points of their arrows. Their massed archers with their copper-tipped arrows were decimating the invading Aztec forces. The exchange, if it had been on a broader scale between north and south, would’ve been potentially an equalizing force. If the Aztec had had llamas from South America, it would’ve been dramatically to their advantage. There is tin in the New World. So no bronze? Why do we not get bronze with copper and tin? They’d never made it, certainly not in Mesoamerica, where everything was stone. They had the worst of both worlds. They could smelt silver and gold to attract the greed of the Spaniards, but they couldn’t smelt iron to hold them off. But before that, you would’ve thought that they would’ve done copper, tin… get bronze. They did have bronze in the Inca world. If the Aztecs had had access to that, again, it would have given them a much more aggressive fighting stance. Bronze can’t stand up against iron, but it’s better than stone. Or obsidian. This is an important part of what happens here, which is that the Europeans are learning from each other, but the natives are not learning from each other. Cortés learned from the conquest of Hispaniola. Of course, he has a ton of improvisation in the way he approaches the conquest of the Aztecs. Then Pizarro knows the whole details of what Cortés did a decade earlier and basically follows the same playbook. It’s not clear to me that the Incas even knew the Aztecs existed. So you just have a much greater accumulation of knowledge than the other one is able to accumulate. They’re closed worlds. They were very insular in their societies. To go further back in time, the only divergence that would’ve given pre-Columbian peoples a real long-term chance to hold off the Europeans is if we reverse the clock another 500 years back to Leif Erikson and the Norse in Greenland. They knew of the existence of North America — Helluland, Markland, and Vinland, as they called them, as they explored them. They tried to colonize the New World. Their numbers were too few, the local resistance was too fierce, and they were forced out. If the Vikings had been able to claim a long-term presence in the New World, they may have been able to drip-feed European technology, European beasts of burden, and above all, European diseases slowly into the Americas, so that 500 years later, when the conquistadors did show up, they’d have met a people who had some resistance to European diseases, knew how to work iron, and were riding horses. If that was the case, all bets are off. That would be such an interesting history. What does the New World look like if the Vikings had actually set up there? That’s very interesting. What was Pizarro’s greatest innovation on Cortés’s strategy? Essentially, the key to it was following the blueprint as closely as possible. Go right to the top, establish a coastal settlement — which Cortés also was very invested in — so you have a secure base to fall back to if what’s happening in the interior is not playing out. A base on the coastline was key. Originally with the conquistadors, but more broadly the Portuguese, British, and French subsequently, they would build those key strongholds on the coast, which, because of their monopoly over nautical technology, meant they were effectively unable to be extricated by any local indigenous population. That’s one thing that people tend to underestimate with the success of European expansion. At the end of the day, they had the fortresses, and again, the long institutional knowledge of what a perfect fortress should look like: those jagged-edged star perimeters and revetments and battlements which maximized firepower. Even Eurasian cultures, even the Qing Chinese, struggled to be able to deal with that. The peoples of the pre-Columbian Americas had no real chance of storming defenses on that kind of a scale. The Inca and Aztecs didn’t even have siege warfare. They didn’t have siege weapons, battering rams, catapults, nothing like that. Once the Europeans dug into fortified positions, it was very hard to move them out of that. Pizarro’s own environment was quite different from that of Cortés. The highlands of Mexico are quite elevated, but the Andes Mountains that the conquistadors were moving into… I’ve been at elevation in the Himalayas and I was struggling to breathe with the altitude sickness. How they were able to move to those heights and then be able to fight battles is really astonishing. With the many dozens of pounds of armor and equipment. It speaks to their physical stamina, honed over many years of hard living, and the core motivations. "This had better be worth it." That’s why Cortés and Pizarro were always first among equals. They had to keep demonstrating success. They had to be the alpha dog in all circumstances, because around them were many ambitious men, some of whom had titles or claims that were just as legitimate as Cortés’s or Pizarro’s. Cortés was always on top of that situation. Pizarro, of course, at the end lost control. He met his end as a rival faction stormed his household. He went down fighting, not against the indigenous people, but against his fellow Spaniards. What I find interesting about these conquests is that once the emperor’s captured — Atahualpa for the Incas and Moctezuma for the Aztecs — what is it that they think is going to happen? Why are they cooperating? What is the end story in which they come out on top? Do they think that the Spanish were going to leave if they gave them enough gold? What is their endgame there? That’s a good question. With Moctezuma II, from the moment when Cortés seized him… Previously, the records of Moctezuma reflect that he was a pitiless autocrat. He centralized all power in the aristocracy. Previously, it used to be possible to work up from outside the circle into the inner circle, but he forbade that. He was almost Stalinist in his authority over his underlings. He was the one who was amping up the ritualistic flower wars into successive campaigns of encirclement and annihilation for the Tlaxcalans. He was, in every sense of the word, an autocrat. Perhaps being told what to do for the first time in his life just led to a complete psychological break, because once Cortés had hold of him, he seems to have pulled a complete 180 switch and become essentially willingly Cortés’s puppet. He was always trying to do what Cortés wanted in a way that would still be beneficial for his people, but would not rock the boat so much in terms of Cortés’s authority. In the end, it wasn’t Cortés who disposed of him. It was his own people, because they realized that he had sold them out and he had to go. His being unilaterally deposed had never happened before in Aztec history. He was the first huey tlatoani to be repudiated by his own people and replaced by someone else. But what explains Atahualpa? What did he think was going to happen? He seemed like he had his wits about him. He did, and the Spanish accounts refer to it. He had a regal bearing and was clearly intelligent. He was adaptive to the situation. He learned to speak Spanish very quickly. He learned to play cards and chess. He was, I think, trying to integrate himself as much as possible. At the same time, he was also reaching out to his own people. He was waiting perhaps for his opportunity to fully mobilize them. Perhaps he just felt he never quite got the opportunity to do that. Bear in mind the deal: he walked into that room and said, "I can fill it up this high with gold and silver," and Pizarro promised he’d be released at that point. Perhaps he actually felt that this was a man of his word. Pizarro in no case was, and had him garroted to death after getting what he wanted. It may just be that he misinterpreted Pizarro as someone who was on the same level, that "kings don’t do this to other kings." But he was dealing with a freebooting entrepreneurist pirate, essentially, not someone operating in good faith at all. Zooming out, it’s interesting to think about what this Spanish New World conquest really accomplished. They’re basically after gold and silver. In a weird way, they’re like paperclippers who don’t really care about this indigenous culture or even the productive capacity of this new continent. They just care about these metals, which are not in and of themselves productive assets. They can’t generate wealth. They’re basically like crypto, if you think about what gold and silver are. They’re just obsessed with this. Initially — we were talking about this yesterday — this helps them fund… Europe’s in a tough spot at this time because of the Ottomans and other forces. The Reconquista has just happened, so the Spanish have been fighting off Arab armies that had control over parts of the Iberian Peninsula. They use this gold and this silver to help pay for those defenses back at home. But if you think about what’s really happening here, there’s no new net wealth that has been generated. It’s just redistribution among Europeans. If they’re using bullion as currency, now the relative ratio of bullion has changed. You get inflation plus some redistribution. It’s basically the government printing more money. That doesn’t create more stuff. At least initially, the Spanish are not getting any new productive capacity out of the New World. What does change is that during this period, the Ming dynasty is in charge of China, and it has really messed up the circulation of paper currency. Nobody trusts it. Every time there’s inflation, they just make up a new currency, and people’s stocks of wealth are totally worthless. Now the Spanish set up the silver mines in the New World, and they ship that silver off to China. China is then sending millions of pieces of porcelain and tons and tons of silk and spices and fine goods to Europe — hundreds of tons of real goods, it might’ve been thousands of tons — in exchange for a medium of exchange, to make up for the fact that they don’t have a reliable means of exchange at the time in the Ming dynasty. That is an important, interesting piece of what the function of the Spanish colonies in the New World is. It’s not generating any net new wealth. In the grand scheme of things, it’s basically helping get Chinese stuff for free, in some sense. The conquistadors really didn’t appreciate what they’d accomplished, many of them. Some of them died doing what they did. The ones who were lucky or good lived long enough to settle down on what were called encomiendas, local estates with many of the local peoples to farm the land for them, a kind of peon serfdom. The later iteration was the bureaucrats. Once the conquistadors had cleared the ground, the bureaucrats would arrive from Spain, and they would begin running and administering what would become Mexico, Peru, South America generally. The conquistadors in South America rebelled against that and had to be put down by force to retain South America within the Spanish Empire. Once that was accomplished, though, the whole process became very integrated into the Spanish imperial orbit, the first empire on which the sun never set. Once the Spanish had discovered and exploited a mountain of silver at Potosí in what’s now Bolivia, they put the last piece of the puzzle in place for true globalization. Initially, the gold and silver was all going east, as you say, into the coffers of King Charles V, and then his son, King Philip II, for their wars of evangelical defense and expansion against the Ottomans, to keep the Netherlands under their control, and ideally, through the Armada, to bring the one true apostolic faith of Catholicism back to England. Most of those ventures, of course, were failures. As you say, the net impact for Spain was ultimately quite negative. By infusing Europe with this enormous expansion of liquidity through the silver that was pouring off the treasure ships arriving in Spain every year, all they were doing was importing inflation, pushing prices up. Real wages in Spain actually fell during this period because, yes, there was excess liquidity, but this meant prices for even staple goods were much higher than they had been previously. The ultimate negative effect was that the seductive drug, essentially, of having silver on tap meant that Spain never developed towards what we call functional capitalism. It never developed capital markets or independent banking systems the way they were emerging in places like the Netherlands and England during this period. Those powers moved ahead while Spain and Portugal were left behind, didn’t really fully industrialize during the 19th century, and were left very rural and poor and backward by the 20th century. Still now they’re catching up from that. So the average Spanish gain from this was quite marginal. "Yes, we can afford the fleets necessary to face off against the Ottomans at Lepanto in 1571," which is good. But in terms of the development of the society, that really plateaued and stayed that way for hundreds of years. The other side of the equation, as you mentioned, is China. Once the Spanish had fully become able to exploit the silver mines of South America, they could then take advantage of their developing knowledge of the Pacific Ocean’s wind currents to send those great ships west to the Philippines, to Manila, their base in the Orient. From there they met with merchants coming from Japan, from Siam, and most of all from China, bringing goods with real practical value in exchange for that extracted silver. As you say, the Spanish are buying goods with value using excess liquidity, and the real benefit from that is not something that’s structurally to the advantage of the country as a whole. What the conquistadors did was historically put the last pieces of the puzzle in place for true globalization. Trade is no longer trans-Eurasian or even trans-Atlantic. It’s trans-global. The networks now affect everybody at some point along the line. The Ming found that out in the mid-1640s when there was a cash crisis, having fully transferred the basis of exchange in their empire to silver. When access to silver was cut off, or at least minimized, for a number of reasons during the first half of the 17th century, the Ming — at the worst possible time, with the Manchu at the gates — could no longer fund their armies. They couldn’t pay to put a military in place to hold them off. So the Manchus moved in at a moment of supreme advantage, blew out the Ming, and replaced them as the new hegemons over the Chinese imperial zone. It’s interesting to contrast the Spanish, who had immediate and outrageous successes in the New World, with the English around the same time, who were setting up the colony in Virginia. There are so many letters from that time where people are complaining about the Virginia Company as literally a waste of English money and lives. "Everybody’s dying. There’s nothing to farm here. There’s nothing to get out of this." In the long run, of course, the British did lose control of the New World, but for a while they did gain these productive new colonies there. I don’t know the right way to think about this. There is this way of thinking about it: maybe because the Spanish had this very quick initial success, they set up a very extractive settlement, which didn’t result in net productive capacity in the long run. Whereas the British, because there was no crazy bullion floating around, were forced to build up some productive capacity, to build up the kinds of institutions that would allow them to make the most use of America, Canada, and so on. You look at the Netherlands and England, the two latecomers to the game of imperialism, Protestant powers marginalized for that reason in Europe. The Netherlands especially has nothing. Natural resources, absolutely zero. In that environment, you’re forced to innovate and think about how you can achieve capital gain and true economic expansion. Everybody wants the quick fix, understandably. We play the lottery for that exact reason. But be careful what you wish for, because you may just actually get it. Those societies that have arrived at the quick fix, whether that’s gold or silver or monocultural crop production of some kind which gives you a monopoly on its provision, become satisfied with that. They become trapped within an environment that exists solely for that purpose, and they don’t develop beyond the essential thresholds that that allows for. Even here in the US, we can look at the difference between the North and the South, that geographic line. Below that line, yes, you can get the quick fix. You can put all your capital into buying slaves and have them work at providing cash crops: tobacco, the original one, indigo and sugar, and of course the big one, cotton. The North didn’t have those advantages. You couldn’t just buy land and then extract the resource from it. You had to think about how to make money. That’s why all the investment money in the North was going into things with long-term productivity: into trade and commerce, into advanced capital markets and the banking system, and of course into industrialization. That all happened in the North. When the rupture came in the 1860s and the North and South went to war, all those advantages were with the North. It wasn’t just population, but the industrial base, the capital markets necessary for it to put in place a winning long-term strategy for actually defeating the South in that conflict. That again is the long-term lesson: having life too easy from the beginning leads to a certain… I don’t want to call it laziness, but lack of vision, perhaps, about alternatives that may, in the longer term, actually be more advantageous. If you could rerun the conquest a thousand times, how often do the Spanish end up in control? What if the Aztecs had learned ironworking from the Vikings? Or the Spanish hadn't brought smallpox? Or Cortés had died earlier? Obviously, with history, counterfactuals are just thought experiments. But with software, you can actually test them. One big problem with traditional testing is that it only covers the distribution that you know. To test for a bug, you first have to imagine it. These tests are sort of like the Flower Wars. The Aztecs' success in these ritual battles against known rivals made them feel like the best military in the world, but they're still unprepared for Cortés.
