Going back to the Aztecs and Incas, one thing that’s remarkable here is just how bad their strategy and tactics often were. For example, because Aztec military conflict was based around capturing victims for sacrifice, there are many cases where Cortés himself was lying on the ground, having been knocked off his horse, but instead of just killing him on the ground and pressing their advantage, the Aztecs were trying to seize him to go have him sacrificed later. This allowed him to get himself out of thorny situations. Another huge mistake that would happen in these conflicts is that, in the traditional way in which these military conflicts would happen, the general would be garnished and plumed with all this extra decoration, which would make him easy to identify on a battlefield. If you have cavalry that can just see, "Here’s where the general is. Let’s go directly to him and kill him," the whole enemy force will rout. It breaks your entire chain of command. You might have 100,000 troops, but then your general dies and everybody else routs. A couple hundred Spaniards defending against this huge rebellion. If the rebellion had just kept attacking at night instead of only attacking during the day, the Spaniards would have never gotten a chance to rest and would have been depleted pretty quickly. Instead, they would let them rest at night. They wouldn’t use their overwhelming numbers to do rotations and attack at different times. Why I find this confusing is that it’s not like the conquistadors arrived at the doorsteps of a bunch of peace-loving hippies who didn’t know anything about war. Aztec society and Inca society were organized around warfare. All adult males were trained to be warriors. It was basically the Sparta of the New World. How do we understand how a very military-focused culture did not have the strategic wherewithal to do this improvisation? Cortés was doing improvisation. Pizarro was doing a bunch of improvisation. Why weren’t they able to do the same thing? There are a lot of factors, one of which is that cultural and religious aspect to it. Warfare was rewarded by an increase in status. Warriors would be recognized for bringing captives back to Tenochtitlan for sacrifice. There was even a hierarchy in terms of the prisoners: four Totonacs were worth one Chichimec, because the Chichimec were a wilder tribe to the north and fought more fiercely than the Totonacs did. To a degree, there’s a certain overlap with European cultures. There was great advantage throughout the feudal era in taking your enemies alive as opposed to killing them, because then you could ransom them back to their families. You could come out of that ahead in terms of at least financial gain. In a way, the Aztec model makes more sense, because sacrificing your enemy means you’ll never see him again, whereas selling your enemy back to his family means he’s up for round two. You might face off against the same guy across a future battlefield. Even kings… The kings of France after the Battles of Poitiers and Pavia were captured and then subject to various indignities. To a degree, there was a certain level of overlap. More holistically, you’re right. Yes, the Aztecs did fight ideally to capture, not to kill. There were multiple episodes when they did have Cortés himself under their knives, but they were so desperate to haul him off that his compatriots, sometimes sacrificing themselves, were able to rescue him. That was an aspect of their warfare ingrained in their culture, in their faith, in the religious expression of the values of an entire people. It’s difficult to tilt that within the framework that we’re talking about. It'd be like asking people here, if we're fighting aliens from another planet, to give up Christianity because there's too much invested in terms of its morality and more effective combat skills would be derived from following some other faith. It was not possible to reorient an entire society in that limited timeframe. The Cuzco situation… To use a famous film example, the movie 300. The Spartans are able to hold the pass at Thermopylae against overwhelming numbers because it’s very, very narrow. Within Cuzco, the surviving conquistadors fell back to two heavily fortified buildings with narrow entrances. The streets of Cuzco are quite narrow as well, which means that even those overwhelming numbers could only be channeled down through those narrow avenues. Those could be blocked relatively effectively, even by the small numbers of conquistadors and their increasing numbers of native allies, who with their metal armor were able to put in place blocking operations that could hold those kinds of frameworks. Beyond that, when the pressure came off, they would unleash their horses, largely through the wider plazas and then the surrounding countryside, looking for isolated detachments to take out. This effect was demoralizing on the Inca. They initially had the advantage. They were rallied by Manco: "We’re going to deal with these barbarians once and for all." As the frustration of the siege drags on, there are more and more defections, men slipping away at night either to switch sides or just go back to their homes and families. Again, this army is huge and very diverse, and many of those recruits are peoples who’ve been subjugated and have no institutional loyalty to the Inca. The bottom line is they need to get home to their farms to get the harvest in before the winter. The nature of warfare in pre-Columbian America, and in Europe for much of its history, was driven by the annual cycle of planting and harvesting the crops. You need the men on their farms to do that, because if they’re not there, you face a very hard year coming up if you don’t have enough crops set aside to get you through the winter and the following year. For all those reasons, the Inca, yes, with those huge numbers, weren’t able to really overwhelmingly apply them at the same time. If they’d caught perhaps the entire conquistador army marching through one of those mountain passes, yes, they may have been able to win a set-piece battle in that environment. The horses can’t charge up the steep slopes. Both ends, if they’re blocked cleverly with boulders and with trees, mean the infantry’s not going anywhere either. You have them essentially pinned down. All the advantages would’ve been useless at that point. Metal armor won’t protect you from a two-ton boulder rumbling from the heights right into your face. On small scales they were able to arrange that. But to get the entire conquistador force in one place at one time like that never happened. So there was basically not enough time to accumulate the strategy? Again, it’s that shockingly short, steep learning curve. There just wasn’t enough time, no. In retrospect, you have these outrageous battles with thousands of deaths on one side and zero deaths on the other side. My understanding is this came as a surprise to the conquistadors as well. How were some hundred-odd men willing to go confront an empire of 10 million people and say, "Your job is to take this over"? It’d be like dropping 100 Marines into 18th-century England and saying, "Okay, you better come out the other end with control over Parliament." How is this a thing that they were willing to do? The classic push-pull factors. They came from a proud land, a new country. Spain had only come together in 1492, and they were aware their history was one of struggle, uniting the peninsula and consummating the Reconquista. They had a proud warrior background, but little to show for it, really. Again, the quality of life, particularly in Extremadura, which is the province in Spain where so many of these conquistadors originated from, was a land of hardship. It was small farms and dusty, unproductive terrain. They were aware of a wider world around them. Like so many men throughout history, they see their only path forward to being anything more than dirt farmers as seeking a career of high risk and potentially even fatal outcomes, but with possible great reward as well. Men have joined armies and militaries and fought in campaigns, not even their own, even other peoples’, for many centuries, millennia now, for that reason. Then there’s the attraction of the New World. Once they’re aware that out there is a whole new land where "it’s possible for even a small man like myself to become something, to become somebody. All I have as an asset is my fighting skills. Yeah, I could keep fighting in the campaigns against the Moors or against the Turks, but those are high-risk, low-return ventures. I’ll be fighting in my home waters, but what will the potential rewards be? These Turks can fight. The message I’m getting from the far side of the Atlantic is that the risks there are much lower and the potential rewards much higher. It means traveling across a vast ocean. It means essentially being very self-reliant and falling in with men who are just as cutthroat as I am." How important is religion in the morale of these soldiers? In retrospect we say, "Okay, they were able to take on huge armies." But they didn’t even know that there was an empire there to take over. They didn’t know where its capital was. They’d never seen it before. They knew nothing about what alliances they would need to maneuver, what size of armies they would need to face, what basic terrain and geography they would need to navigate. I’m still trying to understand how they gained the confidence — you and your couple hundred buddies — to go and take on a huge empire. Did they just think God would make sure that they were okay? How do we understand that? Cortés and Pizarro both had men of the cloth traveling with their expeditions to provide for their spiritual needs. Cortés and Pizarro, in retrospect, would make a great deal of this in their writing. They professed to be outraged, particularly by the Aztec cult of human sacrifice, which was integral to their society. I don’t see any reason why that would be untrue. It would be shocking to actually see that happen. When Moctezuma invited Cortés to the top of one of the two great pyramids in Tenochtitlan, he remarks that the priests were daubed from head to foot in black clay or body paint, and the interior was black with spilled blood and stank abominably. I don’t see any reason why they wouldn’t be genuinely mortified by that. They would take a certain degree of legitimacy from tearing down the ancient idols of these superstitious peoples and raising them up to the one true faith. You can salve your conscience in many ways by doing that: "In real terms I’m here for what I can take, but I am performing the will of God, and therefore I have some absolution when I’m called to account at the end of the day." Men will sometimes seek to find a higher purpose in what are very low endeavors, which is what was playing out at that time. Again, coming to the motivation, the initial conquests were relatively easy. The island populations in the Caribbean — what we now call Puerto Rico, Hispaniola, Cuba — were quite low on the tech tree, were not unified, and did not have a significant military presence. Of course, on islands there’s nowhere for them to run. You could run into the mountains of Cuba, but eventually they would all be hunted down. The feeling was, "The pickings here are pretty easy." The enthusiastic attitude was, "Well, why would it be any different on the mainland?" Of course, they did run up against heavy fighting, and some of them, veterans of wars against the Turks, would say afterwards, "This was some of the hardest fighting I’ve ever been through." By that point they’d seen that the potential rewards were much higher as well. When they arrived at Lake Texcoco, they would marvel as if we had just arrived in Atlantis… to be in this place on this lake with these cities arranged around its edges, and the biggest city of all appearing to float on the water like Venice, except even more dramatic with its high temples. To them, this both awed them and inflamed that greed that they had. Cortés himself admitted as much to Moctezuma’s envoys. He said, "My men and I suffer from a disease of the heart that only gold can cure." So Moctezuma sent him feathered headdresses and all the traditional regalia. He sent him jade and obsidian, which in the Aztec world would have been symbols of great regard. Cortés was happy to put on the headdress, and he sent it back to the king. But the little trinkets of gold that he was getting, that was the real end purpose. The local peoples would talk about how the Spanish were chattering like monkeys as they were picking up this gold, fascinated by it. By the way, Bernal Díaz, who was on Cortés’s expedition and wrote a firsthand account of what happened afterwards, says in his account that in every single town they come across, even small outlying villages of the empire, every temple, every city has tons of human sacrifice remains left over. This is an endemic part of the society that I guess must have horrified the Spanish. But it’s hard to square any sort of humanitarian, even plausible motivation to the Spanish. They were just very willing… I think maybe even worse than almost any other European conquest ever. The Spanish in the New World just massacre huge swaths of innocent women and children. Literal genocides, intentional genocides, not just disease. Crazy amounts of rape and assault. There wasn’t a sense of, "We’re freeing these people from the shackles of oppression," or at least there’s no plausible story you could tell where that’s what you’re doing. They would argue that in the long term it’s in these peoples’ interests. But of course in the short term, as you say, the accounts are unambiguous about this… throwing men and women to those huge hunting dogs that they brought with them for sport, to watch them being torn apart. They could rationalize that in Mesoamerica in reference to that cult of sacrifice, which was endemic, ingrained into the mentalities of those people. There was a regular cycle of sacrifice, and that would peak according to particular crises, major campaigns, or the like. There are accounts — again, these are distorted accounts — of an almost industrial level of human sacrifice, that the parade of prisoners who’d been drugged to keep them pacified stretched from the pyramid complex all the way through to the causeways. One after the other they’d be brought up to the top of the pyramids to the temple complex. Four priests, each would take an arm and a leg, while a fifth one would use an obsidian blade to open an incision and pull out the heart, which they’d offer to the gods. I’ve been to those pyramids, the ones at Teotihuacan. The steps leading up are very, very narrow. You almost have to walk up sideways, deliberately so, because when the heart had been taken out and burned, the body would be tumbled off the far side, and the steps were so steep to allow it to tumble all the way to the bottom. Then the warrior who’d taken that prisoner would take the flesh home to be consumed. It was the consummation of his victory as a warrior to take home, literally internalize, the defeated enemy. This was, as you say, a cycle that the world is better off without. I’m not going to make any excuses for it. But the motivations of those who did that were not selfless. It was at best a beneficial side effect, window dressing on a process of colonization and even literal genocide. It had even less validation in South America, because there was no such tradition at all. The Inca would very occasionally take a virgin sacrifice into the high mountains where she’d be drugged, and you can still find the mummified corpses of these girls, but that was very rare and very specific. There was no ingrained culture of mass human sacrifice at all. Here’s a question I find very interesting. Why is it that the Spaniards are able to weaponize the divisions between the different native forces to their own advantage, but the natives are not able to do the same with the Spaniards? We know there were significant divisions among the Spaniards. Literally, there’s a whole much larger force that is sent over to arrest Cortés. Similarly, we didn’t get into this much, but in Pizarro’s expedition, his second in command, Almagro, is pissed off that Pizarro is getting all the spoils. He comes back, lays siege to Cuzco after Manco Inca has laid siege to Cuzco, and captures it. They were literally fighting, in multiple cases in both these expeditions. The Spanish are fighting each other while they’re taking over a much larger empire. Why is it that Manco Inca or Moctezuma are not able to use these divisions to their own advantage to stay in power? That’s a good question. The critical difference is this is happening for the conquistadors within the Spanish orbit. They’re all Castilians, they’re all Spaniards. Shared common backgrounds for most of them, and their confrontations were personal rivalries within their system. If confronted by an existential crisis, to pit them against each other, I think that would have failed. Their bottom line was they would have united if they felt there was a common cause against them that would have forced them to do that. Whereas for the local peoples, these are differences between entire societies, both the external and internal factors here. Cuitláhuac, who succeeded his brother, Moctezuma II, as huey tlatoani after driving the Spaniards out during La Noche Triste, sent emissaries to the Tlaxcalans to build and expand their own new triple alliance, to come together as native people with a common interest against these outsiders. The Tlaxcalan younger generation thought about it. But the old men who still retained power, who had been fighting against the Aztecs for decades and saw them as, in the end, the existential threat, repudiated this possibility. The Tlaxcalans wanted nothing to do with the Aztecs. "No, we are enemies and always have been and always will be. This is your problem, and maybe you’ll get taken out. No loss to us." They couldn’t overcome the kind of institutional hostilities which go way, way back through their history. The only way that the indigenous people could have played the same game — and to a degree this did happen later — was to play different European powers off against each other. But of course the problem for the Aztecs, for the Inca, was the Spanish were the only game in town. They were the only outsiders they’d encountered. If the English, French, Dutch, even the Ottomans had been players in New World colonization at that very early stage, it may have been possible for local peoples to identify, "Okay, these are different cultures. We can play them off against each other to our advantage." That may have been the game, but at that time, it wasn’t possible. I know you’ve spent a little bit of time thinking about what happened in all these other episodes of colonialism as well. In India, for example, we do have many different European powers. Why was it not possible for the Mughals to play them off each other indefinitely to keep control over India? The Mughals peaked under Aurangzeb at the beginning of the 18th century. Subsequently, his successors didn’t have whatever you call it — the authority, the charisma, the rizz, however you want to define it — to maintain that hegemony which he’d established by almost taking over all of India. The Mughals always at root had the same problem that the Manchu did when they conquered the Ming in the 17th century and built Qing dynasty China to the greatest physical extent it ever had. They were always outsiders. They were not indigenous to the population. In the background, there was always that vague resentment that "we’re being ruled here by foreigners." That was okay while they were going from success to success. But when the momentum halts and it begins to roll backwards, that’s when the local power brokers begin to feel to themselves, "Maybe we have more to gain from breaking away from the empire than we do from remaining within it." Both Mughal India and Manchu China had that kind of fatal defect, as it were, coded into the ultimate basis for their authority. So even without the Europeans, I think the Mughal Empire would have peaked and it would have disintegrated. What would have happened next would be more like a patchwork quilt of different authorities, some larger, some smaller. The Maratha Confederacy I think would have been a major player, but not one that could incorporate the entire subcontinent within its authority. Of course, we look at Chinese history. It’s the constant cycle of the rise of a dynasty, its peak, its decline into anarchy, and then the rise of a new imperial hegemon in the region. That cycle would play out, I think, regardless of whether the Europeans intervened. Specifically in terms of why the British took over at the end, in the bluntest terms, the Royal Navy rules the waves. In terms of projecting power, the Brits were able to force the French out of Canada and out of India because they controlled the sea lanes. Although the French had, in terms of population and military, a larger force, if you can’t project that force, it’s really of no value to you. This is very interesting, because it makes sense why the British could have been one of the major powers in India, where there’s the Marathas, there’s the remainders of the Mughal Empire, there’s many other factions, and then there’s this British outpost. They’ve gotten rid of the French, the British have got Bengal. Why is it that they were able to consolidate where nobody else is able to consolidate? One hypothesis here is that we often talk about advantages in technology, but we don’t talk about advantages in governance or in terms of organization. The East India Company was a company. It had a board that could fire and hire CEOs and lieutenants and whatever. It wasn’t called a CEO, obviously. It wasn’t necessarily the most competent people who were in charge of the East India Trading Company, though they were pretty competent people, Robert Clive and his underlings. You would have instances… For example, I think the nawabs of Bengal right before the British are able to gain control of Bengal, which is the initial tax base that they set up in order to further their conquest. Alivardi Khan is considered to be a pretty good ruler who was able to hold the British at bay. Then he dies, and his grandson is this kind of crazy, psychopathic guy who doesn’t listen to advice and so forth. Because there’s this monarchical tradition across all these principalities in India, there’s just going to be, sooner or later, a bad king, and that king will not be able to stop the British incursion. There are also all these forces around these principalities where it’s, "Who are we going to lend money to? Who are we going to lend our troops to?" They realize there’s a bad king right now. The British seem pretty competent. "I’m going to make an alliance with the British instead." Similarly with the case of the Aztecs and the Incas, and maybe even more so, the overwhelming majority of the troops, the forces that the British are using are Indians, sepoys. This is an underrated thing, being able to delegate to competent people and then being able to replace them one after another if they get killed or if they resign or something. There’s not going to be some crazy succession crisis that creates this anarchy that then somebody else can swoop in and take control. Absolutely. Just to give one example, the British were winning the Hundred Years’ War because the King of France, Charles VI, was insane. He was convinced he was made out of glass, for example. Then the English lost the Hundred Years’ War because their king, Henry VI, was insane, and he would just shut down for months at a time. Mentally, he just couldn’t function. In non-democratic environments, yes, the corporations—for the reasons you outlined, being profit-driven and therefore not loyal to bloodlines or any other kind of extraneous circumstances, but just looking for the most competent leadership and a capacity to change and replace people—are much more flexible than those kind of hierarchical states. Even in many ways, you could argue, in democratic societies the corporations can still outmaneuver them because they’re untied to particular loyalties, and it’s just their brand awareness that they’re trying to push. The British and Dutch were late to the game of imperialism. You wouldn’t give them much of a chance given the differentials, but they unleashed the power of the free market. As you say, the East India Company, the English and Dutch equivalents, the British Hudson’s Bay Company, which is really what made Canada as we understand it today… Those corporations, driven by those motives, acting with almost plenipotentiary powers at vast distances, had the flexibility on the ground to take advantage of local circumstances, to insert themselves into diplomatic arrangements which had persisted for centuries with all the accumulated grievances. Here were the fresh newcomers: "We obviously have skills at what we do." As you say, taking control of the tax revenue of Bengal effectively took control of the entire political orbit. What can you do without taxation? Reorient that towards the shareholders, force the local people to stop producing these crops, even if it means millions starve to death, in order to produce opium to take advantage of that. It was devastating in its impact in political structures that had no real environment where that was part of their established traditions. Even the conquistadors, even the Vikings, if we go back far enough, these were business operations. The Vikings would be men who would assemble with what weapons they had, enough capital upfront to pay for boats to go raiding, to make this profitable by looting churches and monasteries. That’s what the conquistadors were. They were freebooters operating within a capitalist system. These were not desperados of the lowest of the low. Many of them had some education. A lot of them were hidalgos, which is the lowest rung of the petty nobility in Castile, who inherited titles and attitudes, but not much in assets beyond what they could put up as soldiers of fortune. They called themselves compañeros, which is colleagues or companions, not soldados. This whole operation was, if you like, the most raw, unbridled capitalism at its finest, pooling assets to derive enormous profits from very risky ventures. Maybe to close us off, the thing I find really remarkable in these stories… We’ve talked about how lopsided these battles have been, how quickly this conquest proceeded. In the grand scheme of things, the technological advantages that the conquistadors had over these empires are not that shockingly big. As you mentioned, guns at this time are not good enough to actually be that helpful during battle, so it’s basically horses and steel weapons. In fact, I don’t know if we’ve talked about this, but towards the ends of these conquests, or the middles of these conquests, the metal is weighing them down and it’s really hot and stuff. So they’re even reverting to the cotton armor that the natives are using. So it’s not even the armor. It’s really just that they had better metal swords instead of obsidian blades, and that they had horses. That’s enough to let a couple hundred men take over entire empires. You can draw analogies to what might happen in the future. We don’t even have to go there. I just find it remarkable how little it took for them to be so dominant. They could win battles. They could not have won the war, though, at the end without the support of those indigenous allies. So those are all the numbers. Yes, you’re actually right. They would almost keep the helmets to protect the head, but many of them were ditching the metal armor in favor of the local stuff because that was good enough as protection against local weapons. They would not have done that and carried it back to Europe. That would have made them too vulnerable. But here you adapt to the circumstances. You have more freedom of mobility. It’s less exhausting wearing cotton than it is to wear metal. They would make that adaptation. They would even sometimes, for very prized allies, again as a way of integrating them into the Spanish coalition, provide local chiefs and warlords with Spanish weapons, just to emphasize, "You’re so special to me." This was effective in binding them together. They were very, very clever in identifying the local sub-chiefs beneath the Aztec layer of authority who would be receptive to the idea of, "Why are you taking orders from them when you get a better deal from us?" They knew, for example, the Aztec Empire was called the Triple Alliance because it was set up in 1428 by the first huey tlatoani as a coalition between the three city-states: Tenochtitlan, Texcoco, and Tacuba. In 1516, so three years before Cortés arrived in Mesoamerica, the chief, or king, of Texcoco died, and his two sons fought a civil war for who would succeed him. Moctezuma stepped in and gave the title to one of those brothers. So what does the other brother do when the Spanish show up? He immediately sides with them. His own family was on both sides of this war. This is his path to getting what he feels is his due, being the king of Texcoco. In the broader sense, "If I’m under the Spanish versus under the Aztecs, maybe these newcomers will give me a better deal." There was that seductive quality at the elite level. Now, what they didn’t understand—so many of them thinking they were using the Spanish to their advantage—they didn’t appreciate the numbers in which the Spanish and later Europeans would arrive. Their closed worlds didn’t allow them to think that people from such vast distances could come in such huge numbers. In the end, as happened in, for example, Australia and New Zealand, "We’ll become the minority in our own country." The demographic tide they didn’t appreciate in the long term would work fundamentally against them. The analogy to AI there is so obvious it’s not worth spelling out. But that’s actually an excellent place to close. This is such a bizarre and shocking and interesting part of human history, and I appreciate you coming on and telling us all about it. Real pleasure. Thank you for having me.

OpenAI’s Dots lead on the future of ChatGPT

Sources Podcast(90.1K)·45:30·21.3K views·2026-10-01
Alexander EmbiricosDots Product Lead, OpenAI
TL;DROpenAI has launched "Dots"—proactive, persistent personal AI agents equipped with their own cloud computers and unified context across apps like Slack and voice—marking the transition into "Active Intelligence" where agents propose actions rather than waiting for prompts.

TL;DR

OpenAI has launched "Dots"—proactive, persistent personal AI agents equipped with their own cloud computers and unified context across apps like Slack and voice—marking the transition into "Active Intelligence" where agents propose actions rather than waiting for prompts.

The Evolution to Active Intelligence & The "Dots" Form Factor

  • Beyond Passive Chat: Alexander Embiricos noted that while Chat and coding agents are powerful, they require users to drive every action, functioning like a principal engineer who refuses to check Slack or Jira.
  • The "Dots" Breakthrough: Named "Active Intelligence" (OpenAI's third major era), Dots are designed to be persistent, operate over long horizons, and handle open-ended tasks (e.g., "Stay on top of this feedback channel").
  • Cloud Computers over Local Control: OpenAI debated building local agents (like the *slash-goal* feature in Codex) versus cloud-embodied agents; they chose cloud-hosted computers with unique identities so agents can work autonomously without relying on the user's local hardware.
  • Embodiment and Unified Context: Giving agents identities (e.g., prefixed with user handles like `AE-dot`) makes them intuitive for humans to interact with across multiple surfaces (commute voice calls, Slack threads, and desktop apps) while maintaining a single, unified context.
  • Single Agent vs. Multiple Agents: OpenAI discovered that letting users create multiple agents with whimsical names caused cognitive overload in shared environments like Slack; most consumers will likely want just one primary agent that feels like an extension of themselves.

Spaces: Collaborative Workspaces for Humans and Agents

  • Bridging Messaging and Docs: While messaging works well for quick chats, long-form outputs require a dedicated interface. OpenAI built Space, a collaborative workspace designed for equal-weight human and agent collaboration.
  • Agentic Workflows: Users can continuously tag their Dot in a document to pull data, flesh out sections, check off completed tasks, or ping teammates.
  • HTML & Agent-First Design: Unlike traditional documents optimized strictly for human typing, Spaces leverage modern HTML and CSS to host live interactive charts, data visualizations, and clickable UI prototypes that agents can keep up to date.

Safety, Trust, and Permission Architecture

  • Conservative Rollout: To ensure Dots are the most capable and trustworthy agents, OpenAI initially rolled them out strictly to Pro, Business, and Enterprise tiers (their most AI-literate audiences) with highly conservative default behaviors.
  • Default Confirmation Policy: By default, Dots perform proactive research using connected tools but never take action without user confirmation (e.g., asking before moving a conflicting calendar meeting or purchasing an item).
  • Defense-in-Depth Architecture: Running on the Codex harness, the system uses GPT-6 Astra combined with Auto-Review—a secondary model acting as a police officer to monitor base-model behavior against user rules—alongside full sub-agent auditability logs.
  • Ecosystem and Web-Bot Standards: Rather than bypassing web platforms aggressively, OpenAI is taking an ecosystem-first approach, collaborating with partners like Cloudflare on `web-bot auth` so agents explicitly declare themselves as bots.

Pricing and the Future Roadmap

  • Flat Monthly Contractor Pricing: OpenAI is moving away from traditional strict rate limits for Dots, designing pricing more like a responsive human contractor. Users pay a flat monthly fee for 24/7 availability, with the agent intelligently managing its own compute costs (e.g., running heavy tasks overnight or switching to more cost-efficient models as limits approach). Dot usage is separate from main Codex limits.
  • Coexistence with ChatGPT: ChatGPT (with 1.2 billion weekly users) remains the consumer assistant for everyone; OpenAI plans to eventually merge the Work tab into Chat and bring Dot capabilities all the way down to free users over time.
  • Specialist Dots: While personal assistant Dots act as extensions of individuals, OpenAI is developing Specialist Dots centrally managed by IT and designed to accelerate entire enterprise teams or workflows.

Notable Quotes

*"Even if you have an incredibly powerful coding agent, it's kind of like you have this like incredible principal engineer who refuses to check Slack, refuses to check linear and basically only does things when they're told by you."* — Alexander Embiricos
*"As soon as you start... designing software to be embodied or in some future... building robots that are humanoid, you start to get to reuse a lot of really excellent design thinking that has already been done elsewhere in the world."* — Alexander Embiricos
*"Your Dot should be available 24/7, and there should be no way for it to not be available 24/7... We want your Dot is available 24/7, you pay a flat monthly amount to have access to your Dot."* — Alexander Embiricos

We need to be super thoughtful and careful about how we approach that. All the interplay between those things right now I think can be really sanded down and made super smooth. Yeah. so I want to do that as well. the other really interesting thing that we haven’t talked about, I think yet, is Specialist Dots. Yes. Right? And so we have this idea of like, there’s basically like two things going on, we have assistant Dots that’s everyone has an extension of themselves, and then separately, you know, if you’re you’re an organization and you have some function or workflow that you would like to have more like more automated, you may not want that being done by individuals who have their own personal Dots taking on that work. You may want to actually like control that centrally, right? And so you can create specialist Dots which specialize in doing a particular thing and which are centrally managed by IT, which have their own identity completely separate from any sort of individual user and which are accelerating an entire team or an entire organization. And so I think the other thing that I’m really excited about over the upcoming months is bringing specialist Dots to life, and entering this world where it’s like we have us, we have our our personal assistant Dots, and then we have a system Dots much more broadly deployed within an enterprise. Suno's Mikey Shulman on the AI Music Landscape

Bloomberg Live(197.0K)·20:17·2.3K views·2026-10-01
Mikey ShulmanCo-Founder & CEO, Suno Ashley CarmanReporter, Bloomberg Lucas ShawReporter, Bloomberg
TL;DRSuno CEO Mikey Shulman discusses the company's evolution from a controversial generative music tool into a broader "creative entertainment" platform, detailing industry partnerships, upcoming speech products, and a vision for deeply interactive fan-artist music experiences.

TL;DR

Suno CEO Mikey Shulman discusses the company's evolution from a controversial generative music tool into a broader "creative entertainment" platform, detailing industry partnerships, upcoming speech products, and a vision for deeply interactive fan-artist music experiences.

Company Vision and Product Ecosystem

  • Creative Entertainment over Passive Consumption: Mikey Shulman frames Suno's primary purpose as providing a wholesome creative experience for everyone rather than competing directly with traditional streaming consumption models.
    • The platform appeals to both Grammy winners refining their work and grandmothers making music for fun.
    • Average user sessions on Suno are about 30 minutes long.
  • Blurring the Line Between Hobbyist and Professional: Mikey Shulman notes that while professional artists, producers, and songwriters widely use Suno bits and pieces in smash hits, the company deliberately avoids rigid distinctions between hobbyists and professionals.
  • Expansion into Speech: Mikey Shulman announced the rollout of Suno's first hyper-realistic, hyper-expressive speech product designed for creative use cases like bringing poems to life or creating expressive audio content.

Artist Adoption and Industry Partnerships

  • Overcoming Industry Hesitation: Mikey Shulman explains that public artist adoption is low due to social media noise and fear of being the first to stick their neck out against prevailing industry controversy, though industry usage behind the scenes is widespread.
  • Co-Creative Products with Rightsholders: Mikey Shulman highlights that Suno has secured deals with partners like Warner and BMG to build models and products that let users play with, remix, sample, and mash up music from their favorite artists.
  • The Litigious Landscape: Ashley Carman and Lucas Shaw point out ongoing copyright disputes and lawsuits from holdouts Universal Music and Sony regarding unlicensed training data, to which Mikey Shulman responds that the music industry is historically litigious and will eventually come around once the growth potential and new revenue streams are realized.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Mikey Shulman predicts that within 5 years, fans will be able to buy interactive albums from their favorite artists, featuring fill-in-the-blank or adaptable portions of songs designed to be personalized and co-created.
  • Mikey Shulman predicts that interactive fan-made song versions will eventually bridge the digital and physical worlds, with artists playing audience-customized tracks during live hometown concerts.
  • Mikey Shulman predicts that 3 years from now, the number of Suno songs created every day will be "infinite" (many millions, comparable to the daily volume of photos taken).
  • Mikey Shulman predicts that in the future, whether a song is generated by AI will become entirely irrelevant, comparing the trajectory to the acceptance of Auto-Tune over the last 20 years.

The unstoppable green revolution: Adam Tooze on the "most dramatic story in the history of energy”

World Economic Forum(1.1M)·35:44·3.1K views·2026-10-01
Adam ToozeDirector of the European Institute, Columbia University
TL;DRDespite geopolitical conflict and political pushback, the green energy transition is unstoppable, driven by plunging costs of Chinese renewables hardware that offer emerging economies a viable alternative to imported fossil fuels.
Historical Precedent for Shocks: According to...

TL;DR

Despite geopolitical conflict and political pushback, the green energy transition is unstoppable, driven by plunging costs of Chinese renewables hardware that offer emerging economies a viable alternative to imported fossil fuels.

Geopolitical Realities & Fossil Fuel Vulnerability

  • Historical Precedent for Shocks: According to Bloomberg’s Javier Blas cited by Adam Tooze, the recent energy instability marks the 14th major shock to the fossil fuel system since the early 1970s.
  • Geopolitical Volatility: Recent conflicts—such as the US and Israeli strikes on Iran and the closure of the Strait of Hormuz—demonstrate that the global economy does not operate on a baseline of cooperative stability, exposing deep flaws in traditional international relations frameworks.
  • Asymmetric Global Impact: While wealthy nations absorbed energy disruptions relatively well, lower-income regions like Thailand, India, Pakistan, and Bangladesh suffered severely, with future fertilizer price spikes threatening upcoming crop cycles.
  • Diversification Away from Oil and Gas: High energy shocks are prompting nations to pivot away from imported hydrocarbons, leveraging American LNG or surging imports of Chinese green electrotech (solar panels, batteries, and EVs).

China’s Green Industrial Strategy

  • The Scale of the Transition: China's historic industrial surge from 2000 onward—driven by 7% to 9% annual growth—has plateaued since the mid-2010s and COVID-19, with greenhouse gas emissions now potentially on the downslope toward Xi Jinping’s double-carbon pledges.
  • Modest Subsidies for Massive Impact: Best Western estimates peg the total state subsidy for China's world-changing EV industry at $150 billion over 15 years (roughly $10 billion per year)—an amount comparable to Western spending but applied with far greater consistency.
  • State Commitment vs. Market Incentives: China's success stems from top-level political signaling (such as Xi Jinping's "two mountain theories") combined with unyielding entrepreneurial energy, even though companies currently make very narrow profit margins on photovoltaics, batteries, and EVs.
  • Security and Dependence Anxieties: European and global hesitation to adopt Chinese green tech stems from fears of new monopolies, lack of transparency regarding potential kill switches in inverters, and China’s strategic alignment with Russia during the Ukraine war.

European Policy Paradoxes & Industrial Dilemmas

  • The Automotive Dead End: European car manufacturers failed to prepare for decarbonization 15 години ago, focusing instead on perfecting turbodiesel engines while corporate owners (particularly in Germany) milked firms for tens of billions of euros in dividends.
  • Protection of Legacy Jobs: The European auto sector employs over 12 million people across its broader supply chain, making the influx of cheap Chinese EVs an existential political and economic challenge.
  • Flawed Energy Pricing Models: Europe’s unbundled electricity market sets marginal prices using expensive gas and coal plants, creating the false paradox that green energy drives up consumer bills, unlike China's strategy of driving electricity prices directly down.
  • The Cost of Entry: European trade barriers on Chinese vehicles are designed to force local consumers to pay above-market prices to protect domestic manufacturing employment.

Development in the Global South

  • Leapfrogging Traditional Grids: In regions like Africa, where traditional grid infrastructure is absent, affordable solar panels, batteries, and inverters (supported by initiatives from the African Development Bank and World Bank) are electrifying millions of homes for a fraction of historical costs.
  • Micro-Scale Modernization: A few $10 billion can electrify 300 million people in Africa, granting immediate access to cellular telephony, lighting, and electric tuk-tuks, bypassing traditional industrialization pathways that were stalled by 1970s oil shocks and IMF programs.
  • Countering the "Flying Geese" Doom: While automation raises questions about manufacturing migration, countries like Bangladesh (in textiles) and Vietnam (serving as an entrepôt and adopting green tech locally) prove that dispersion of industrial technology to lower-cost nations is still happening.

The Overlooked Threat of Pandemics

  • Catastrophic Potential: Adam Tooze identifies unchecked pandemic risks as the greatest threat to humanity that leaders choose to ignore, noting that COVID-19 caused a 20% economic downturn in 3 to 4 months—a scale of disruption far exceeding most climate models.
  • Systemic Complacency: Despite historical warnings and organizations like the WHO struggling with depleted budgets (such as during recent Ebola outbreaks), global leadership continues to underinvest in first-stage vaccine development and pandemic preparedness.

Notable Quotes

"This is the 14th major shock to the fossil fuel system since the early 1970s. So, the idea that fossil fuels are the reliable source of power... is profoundly discredited at this point." — Adam Tooze
"Best Western estimates of the total subsidy for the EV industry, which is a world changing event, are in the order of 150 billion dollars over 15 years. So that's 10 10 billion dollars a year. This is not radically out of kilter with what other societies have been thinking about spending in terms of green industry." — Adam Tooze
"To get to the kind of casualty numbers that you had from COVID, which was a mild pandemic in a climate scenario, you have to go far, far, far out on the spectrum of disasters to come even close. To see the death, the mass chaos, disruption of life that we saw all across the world. And the disruption to the global economy. To a 20% down in the space of 3 months, 3-4 months. 20% down. That is off the charts of even the worst climate models." — Adam Tooze

Watch CNBC's full interview with Minneapolis Fed President Neel Kashkari

Steve LiesmanReporter, CNBC Neel KashkariPresident, Minneapolis Fed

TL;DR

Minneapolis Fed President Neel Kashkari noted that while consumer resilience continues to surprise the Fed, inflation remains too high at approximately 3% and requires continued vigilance. Kashkari also discussed the economic impact of the massive AI infrastructure build-out, noting it has pushed up his short-term estimate of the neutral federal funds rate.

Inflation, Economic Resilience, and Supply Shocks

  • Inflation Status: Inflation remains too high, running at approximately a 3% rate and elevated for more than 5 years.
  • Consumer Resilience: The national unemployment rate is 4.1%, and despite high accumulated prices, consumers continue spending and maintaining resilience.
  • Rolling Supply Shocks: Over the past 5+ years, the economy has faced rolling supply shocks—including COVID-19 disruptions, commodity shocks from Russia's invasion of Ukraine, tariffs and trade wars, and the conflict involving Iran—raising the risk that inflation becomes embedded in decision-making.
  • 1970s Parallels: AI-assisted reviews of 1970s FOMC transcripts revealed striking similarities in policymakers diagnosing inflation as merely a supply shock; however, today's environment lacks a wage-price spiral, distinguishing it from the 1970s.

Monetary Policy, Neutral Rates, and Market Signals

  • Short-Term Neutral Rate Revisions: The prolonged resilience of the economy has caused Kashkari to continuously question how tight monetary policy actually is and to slowly revise his estimate of the neutral federal funds rate (previously raised to 3.25% in September).
  • Elevated Neutral Rate Drivers: Massive investments going into data centers, power, water, and related infrastructure have created new demand for investment capital, pushing up the neutral rate temporarily.
  • The Two-Year Yield Gap: The two-year Treasury yield recently ended around 4.88%, while the median dot in the Summary of Economic Projections implies a two-year rate of approximately 4.05% to 4.06%—representing the largest gap ever recorded between the two and the implied rate.
  • Kashkari's SEP Outlook: In the September SEP, Kashkari jotted down in pencil two rate hikes for the year (one already completed, one remaining) and another hike for 2027, though all real-time assessments remain subject to incoming GDP and consumer spending data.
  • Balance Sheet Strategy: The balance sheet is best utilized as a tool at the zero lower bound of the federal funds rate to provide stimulus by purchasing longer-dated securities; shrinking the balance sheet toward an ample reserves regime makes sense, though the Fed must remain cautious not to fall below banking sector needs (as occurred in 2019).

The AI Boom, Capital Allocation, and Productivity Risks

  • Monetary Policy as a Blunt Instrument: Higher interest rates serve as the mechanism for capital reallocation in a market economy; while it pressures sectors like housing, capital is simultaneously channeled toward building data centers.
  • Resource Intensity: AI computation is intensely resource-intensive (e.g., generating a 6-second video of children from 1 minute of processing time consumes as much electricity as running a clothes dryer for an hour).
  • Productivity Paradox: While non-tech CEOs are experimenting, the fruits have not yet borne out on the bottom line, and historical inventions like electricity and the internal combustion engine did not generate the absurd 10% real GDP growth figures touted by Silicon Valley optimists.
  • Potential Economic Hangovers: If the massive investment in AI infrastructure fails to deliver expected productivity gains, the primary risk is an economic hangover and malinvestment rather than an immediate systemic banking crisis (given private credit and hyperscaler equity structures).

Labor Market Dynamics

  • Break-Even Job Growth: As immigration numbers have declined, the break-even rate of monthly job creation needed to keep up with working-age population growth has dropped from 100,000–125,000 down to roughly 30,000 to 50,000.
  • Prime-Age Strength: While aggregate labor force participation has declined due to society's aging, prime-age (25 to 54 years old) labor force participation and employment-to-population ratios remain healthy.
  • Inflation vs. Recession: Regional outreach (such as conversations with 9th District labor unions representing low-income service workers) highlights that for vulnerable populations, inflation can feel worse than a recession because inflation impacts every part of an individual's support network simultaneously.

Predictions & Forward Calls

  • Neel Kashkari's Base Case on Supply Shocks: Will continue to set monetary policy assuming the current environment of rolling geopolitical and trade supply shocks persists until proven otherwise.
  • Neel Kashkari's AI Adoption Timeline Prediction: Predicts that AI will take much longer than optimists think to achieve widespread, transformative utility across mainstream American production cycles.

  • > The longer this goes on, the longer the economy is proving to be resilient in the face of all these shocks, the more I am questioning how tight is monetary policy. — Neel Kashkari
  • > What if AI is just not as useful as we hope it is? This is a massive amount of investment going in betting that this is all going to be transformational. What if it's not? — Neel Kashkari

The $6.7 trillion balance sheet. There's a task force for that. your take on whether or not the balance sheet ought to be a tool used more often as part of policy. >> I don't think so. I think the balance sheet is best as it It was originally used once you got to the zero lower bound with the federal funds rate, the question was is the Fed out of ammunition? And the answer is no. In those moments, it can be a tool to provide more stimulus by buying longer dated securities and effectively pushing down the longer end of the yield curve. Kind of a commitment device that if we're buying up assets, we're probably not going to be raising the federal funds rate at the same time. So, I think in those zero lower bound moments, I think it's still a useful policy tool to be considered. Is there utility in that? >> You know, it it's it's a quite a complicated question because the demand for reserves, the the banking system based on its own needs for its customers and the growth of the economy and regulation, the banking system demands reserves from the Federal Reserve for their own functioning. Why One Internet Pioneer Thinks the Original Model Broke

a16z(299.0K)·45:06·5.6K views·2026-10-01
Joel de la GarzaHost, a16z Barrett LyonFounder, DoxxNet
TL;DRBarrett Lyon and Joel de la Garza discuss the stagnation of internet protocol design, the rise of pervasive digital surveillance, and how Lyon’s new venture, Doxnet, aims to rebuild a private, peer-to-peer mesh network from the silicon up to protect ...
users from trackers,...

TL;DR

Barrett Lyon and Joel de la Garza discuss the stagnation of internet protocol design, the rise of pervasive digital surveillance, and how Lyon’s new venture, Doxnet, aims to rebuild a private, peer-to-peer mesh network from the silicon up to protect users from trackers, corporate data-harvesting, and centralized intermediaries.

The State of the Internet and Modern Surveillance

  • Erosion of Original Internet Ideals: The internet's original architecture was built by free-speech maximalists on open, decentralized concepts, but it has shifted into a tightly controlled chokehold managed by a handful of entities.
  • Carrier-Grade NAT: Lyon identifies carrier-grade NAT as one of the biggest stains on the modern internet, actively destroying applications' ability to perform innovative point-to-point tasks.
  • Creeping Domestic Censorship: State-level implementation of know-your-customer (KYC) rules and age-verification laws are forcing networks to solve identity problems they were never designed to handle, resulting in massive accidental collateral blocks (such as a French piracy policy mistakenly blacking out a major carrier via Cisco Umbrella).
  • The Ubiquity of Ad Tracking: Beyond state surveillance, commercial app trackers (including telemetry metadata on platforms like WhatsApp and Apple) accumulate exhaustive behavioral profiles—recording coffee habits, wake-up times, income levels, and major life milestones—that can instantly be queried and synthesized by modern LLMs.

Doxnet: Rebuilding the Stack for Privacy

  • Origins of the Name: The project draws its name from Lyon's high school hacker handle ("Dox") and a high school enterprise (Doxnet) where he and a friend sold shell accounts for $5 a month to roughly 1,000 users.
  • Architecture & Infrastructure:
    • Full-Stack Ownership: Doxnet owns its hardware from software down to the wire, operating 26 sites globally with redundant routing.
    • AI-Driven Operations: The network runs entirely without traditional network administrators or sysadmins, managed locally via AI agents rather than third-party inference providers to eliminate insider threats.
    • Corporate Governance: Operations are split across a US-based entity and a Swiss company handling European operations to ensure rigorous regional privacy governance.
  • Mesh Network and P2P Capabilities:
    • Bypasses traditional server relays used by apps like WhatsApp, Signal, iMessage, and Telegram (which Lyon characterizes as deceptive server-routed architectures).
    • Enables direct, encrypted peer-to-peer communication, phone calls, video calls, and file transfers with no file size limits (successfully transmitting 20GB files across a private network point-to-point with post-quantum encryption).
    • Requires only a human-verification puzzle to issue a privacy token; accounts operate without names, phone numbers, email addresses, or permanent identifiers.

Critiques of Existing Privacy Tools

  • The Illusion of Free VPNs: Most consumer VPNs merely act as basic entry and exit points leased from third-party landlords. Lyon warns that "if it's free, you are the product," noting that many foreign-operated VPNs have opaque ownership structures and potential ties to intelligence-crime syndicates.
  • Historical Precedents: Lyon highlights law enforcement stings—such as the FBI building and marketing a secure messaging app exclusively to organized crime groups—to demonstrate the hidden dangers of trusting centralized "private" messaging tools.

Barrett Lyon’s Track Record in Internet Infrastructure

  • Prolexic Technologies: Founded after Lyon formalized his high school DDoS-mitigation techniques (presented at Sans and Google) to protect businesses, e-commerce sites, and gambling platforms from extortion and cybercrime. The cloud-scale DDoS defense service eventually became Akamai Prolexic.
  • DefenseNet and Big Gravity: Later founded DefenseNet (acquired as F5 Silverline) and Big Gravity, one of the earliest video-centric content delivery networks (CDNs) designed to optimize bandwidth and progressive video streaming.
  • The Habat Project (Obby): A philosophical art and data-mapping project born from a $50 bet while recovering from being hit by a bicycle-riding car. The project mapped the internet and became part of the permanent collection at the Museum of Modern Art (MoMA) and the Boston Museum of Science.
  • Cybercrime Sagas and Burning Man: Authored a book detailing the real-world pursuit and takedown of aggressive Russian cybercriminals who were destroying client Christmases via extortion. Additionally, Lyon co-founded the Mine Warrior camp and art car culture at Burning Man, attending roughly 14 times.
